\textit{Lecture, Fri Oct 9.}

\begin{propbox}[{Abelian Quotients and Commutators}]\label{prop:abelian-quotients-commutators}
Let $N \trianglelefteq H$. The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $H/N$ is abelian;
    \item every commutator $ghg^{-1}h^{-1}$ ($g, h \in H$) lies in $N$;
    \item $D(H) \subseteq N$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{lecture 10/9}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $gN$ and $hN$ commute iff $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in N$.\par
\textbf{(1) $\Leftrightarrow$ (2).} For $g, h \in H$, compute in $H/N$:
\[ (gN)(hN)(gN)^{-1}(hN)^{-1} = ghg^{-1}h^{-1}N. \]
So $gN$ and $hN$ commute iff this is the identity $N$ of $H/N$, i.e.\ iff $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in N$. Ranging over all $g, h$ gives the equivalence.

\textbf{(2) $\Leftrightarrow$ (3).} $D(H)$ is the subgroup generated by the commutators (Definition~\ref{def:commutator-subgroup}), i.e.\ the smallest subgroup containing them. So a subgroup $N$ contains all commutators iff it contains $D(H)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{$D(H)$ Is the Smallest Normal Subgroup with Abelian Quotient}]\label{rem:derived-smallest-normal}
With $N = D(H)$, the proposition recovers The Abelianization Is Abelian (Proposition~\ref{prop:abelianization-abelian}). Conversely, it says that $D(H)$ is contained in every normal subgroup with abelian quotient. So $H^{\mathrm{ab}} = H/D(H)$ is the largest abelian quotient of $H$. This is the fact used in \ref{sec:solvable}: in a chain with abelian quotients, every commutator of $G_j$ lies in $G_{j-1}$.
\end{rembox}


% ============================================================================
% SECTION: S_3, A_n AND GL_n (Examples revisited)
% ============================================================================

\section{Solvable Groups}\label{sec:solvable}

\textit{Reference: not covered in the Pinter chapters used so far.}

\begin{defbox}[{Solvable Group}]\label{def:solvable-group}
A group $G$ is \textbf{solvable} if there is a chain of subgroups
\[ \{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq G_1 \trianglelefteq G_2 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G \]
in which each $G_{j-1}$ is normal in $G_j$ and each quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is abelian.
\srcline{WS 9}
\end{defbox}

\begin{rembox}[{Normal in the Next Term Only}]\label{rem:normal-in-next-term}
Each $G_{j-1}$ is required to be normal in $G_j$, not in $G$. For example, in the chain for $S_4$ below, $K$ is normal in $A_4$ (and happens to be normal in $S_4$ as well), but in general the terms of such a chain need not be normal in $G$. Normality is not transitive: $A \trianglelefteq B \trianglelefteq C$ does not imply $A \trianglelefteq C$ (Example~\ref{ex:normality-not-transitive}, PS 6.1). Every abelian group is solvable, via the chain $\{e\} \trianglelefteq G$.
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{$S_3$ and $S_4$ Are Solvable}]\label{prop:s3-s4-solvable}
The chains
\[ \{e\} \trianglelefteq A_3 \trianglelefteq S_3 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \{e\} \trianglelefteq K \trianglelefteq A_4 \trianglelefteq S_4 \]
have the required properties, so $S_3$ and $S_4$ are solvable.
\srcline{WS 9.1}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Each step is normal, with quotient of order $2$, $3$ or $4$, hence abelian.\par
\emph{$S_3$.} $A_3$ is normal in $S_3$ (index $2$, Proposition~\ref{prop:subgroups-index-2-normal}), and $A_3/\{e\} \cong A_3 \cong \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$ and $S_3/A_3 \cong \{\pm 1\}$ are abelian (the sign, Theorem~\ref{thm:sign-homomorphism}, and the First Isomorphism Theorem~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem}).

\emph{$S_4$.} $K \trianglelefteq A_4$ and $A_4/K \cong \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$ (The Pair-Partition Homomorphism, Proposition~\ref{prop:pair-partition-homomorphism-s4-to-s3}); $A_4 \trianglelefteq S_4$ (index $2$) with $S_4/A_4 \cong \{\pm 1\}$; and $K/\{e\} \cong K$ is abelian, having order $4$ (Groups of Order $4$ Are Abelian, Proposition~\ref{prop:groups-order-4-abelian}). All three quotients are abelian.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof (direct check of the chain for $S_4$)]
\textit{(Not from lecture; from the student's worksheet solutions.)} The chain for $S_3$ is checked as in the first proof. For $S_4$, with $K = \{e,\ (1\,2)(3\,4),\ (1\,3)(2\,4),\ (1\,4)(2\,3)\}$ (Definition~\ref{def:klein-four-group}):
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{$K$ is abelian.} Each non-identity element of $K$ is its own inverse, and the product of two distinct non-identity elements, in either order, is the third, e.g.\ $(1\,2)(3\,4) \cdot (1\,3)(2\,4) = (1\,4)(2\,3) = (1\,3)(2\,4) \cdot (1\,2)(3\,4)$. So $K/\{e\} \cong K$ is abelian.
    \item \emph{$K \trianglelefteq S_4$, hence $K \trianglelefteq A_4$.} For $\sigma \in S_4$ and distinct $a, b, c, d \in \{1, 2, 3, 4\}$, $\sigma\,(a\,b)(c\,d)\,\sigma^{-1} = (\sigma(a)\,\sigma(b))(\sigma(c)\,\sigma(d))$ (Conjugation Relabels the Entries, Lemma~\ref{lem:conjugation-relabels-entries}). So conjugation permutes the three products of two disjoint transpositions, and $\sigma K \sigma^{-1} \subseteq K$ for every $\sigma \in S_4$ (Definition~\ref{def:normal-subgroup}), in particular for every $\sigma \in A_4$. Also $K \subseteq A_4$, since its elements are even.
    \item \emph{$A_4/K$ is abelian.} $|A_4/K| = 12/4 = 3$ (Lagrange, Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}) is prime, so $A_4/K$ is cyclic (Groups of Prime Order, Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}), hence abelian.
    \item \emph{$S_4/A_4$ is abelian.} $A_4 \trianglelefteq S_4$ (index $2$, Proposition~\ref{prop:subgroups-index-2-normal}), and $|S_4/A_4| = 2$ is prime, so $S_4/A_4$ is cyclic, hence abelian.
\end{itemize}
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{Subgroups of Solvable Groups Are Solvable}]\label{prop:subgroups-solvable}
If $G$ is solvable and $S \leq G$, then $S$ is solvable.
\srcline{WS 9.2; lecture 10/7, 10/9}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Intersect the chain with $S$; each new quotient embeds in $G_j/G_{j-1}$.\par
Let $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ be a chain as in the definition. We show that
\[ \{e\} = S \cap G_0 \;\leq\; S \cap G_1 \;\leq\; \cdots \;\leq\; S \cap G_N = S \]
is such a chain for $S$.

\emph{Normality.} Let $g \in S \cap G_{j-1}$ and $h \in S \cap G_j$. Then $hgh^{-1} \in G_{j-1}$, since $h \in G_j$, $g \in G_{j-1}$ and $G_{j-1} \trianglelefteq G_j$; and $hgh^{-1} \in S$, since $g, h \in S$. So $hgh^{-1} \in S \cap G_{j-1}$, and $S \cap G_{j-1} \trianglelefteq S \cap G_j$.

\emph{Abelian quotients.} The composite $\alpha: S \cap G_j \hookrightarrow G_j \twoheadrightarrow G_j/G_{j-1}$ is a homomorphism with kernel $(S \cap G_j) \cap G_{j-1} = S \cap G_{j-1}$ (Restricting a Homomorphism, Lemma~\ref{lem:restricting-homomorphism}). By the First Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem}), $(S \cap G_j)/(S \cap G_{j-1}) \cong \operatorname{Im}\alpha$, a subgroup of the abelian group $G_j/G_{j-1}$, hence abelian.

\emph{Second proof of the abelian step (lecture 10/9).} By Abelian Quotients and Commutators (Proposition~\ref{prop:abelian-quotients-commutators}), it suffices to show that every commutator of $S \cap G_j$ lies in $S \cap G_{j-1}$. Let $g, h \in S \cap G_j$. Then $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in G_{j-1}$, because $g, h \in G_j$ and $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is abelian; and $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in S$, because $g, h \in S$. Hence $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in S \cap G_{j-1}$.

\emph{Third proof of the abelian step (Second Isomorphism Theorem).} \textit{(Not from lecture; from the student's worksheet solutions.)} Apply the Second Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:second-isomorphism-theorem}) in the group $G_j$, with the subgroup $S \cap G_j$ in the role of $H$ and the normal subgroup $G_{j-1}$ in the role of $N$. Since $(S \cap G_j) \cap G_{j-1} = S \cap G_{j-1}$,
\[ (S \cap G_j)/(S \cap G_{j-1}) = (S \cap G_j)\big/\big((S \cap G_j) \cap G_{j-1}\big) \cong (S \cap G_j)G_{j-1}/G_{j-1} \;\leq\; G_j/G_{j-1}, \]
a subgroup of an abelian group, hence abelian.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{Quotients of Solvable Groups Are Solvable}]\label{prop:quotients-solvable}
If $G$ is solvable and $\pi: G \to Q$ is a surjective homomorphism (for instance, the projection onto a quotient group $G/N$), then $Q$ is solvable. For a chain $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ as in the definition, the natural candidate chain is
\[ \{e\} = \pi(G_0) \;\leq\; \pi(G_1) \;\leq\; \cdots \;\leq\; \pi(G_N) = Q. \]
\srcline{WS 9.3; lecture 10/9}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Push the chain forward by $\pi$; images of commutators of $G_j$ lie in $\pi(G_{j-1})$.\par
\textit{(Lecture 10/9.)} Let $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ be a chain with abelian quotients. Each $\pi(G_j)$ is a subgroup of $Q$, being the image of a subgroup under a homomorphism (Images and Preimages of Subgroups, Proposition~\ref{prop:images-preimages-subgroups}). Also $G_{j-1} \subseteq G_j$ gives $\pi(G_{j-1}) \subseteq \pi(G_j)$; $\pi(G_0) = \{\pi(e)\} = \{e\}$; and $\pi(G_N) = \pi(G) = Q$, since $\pi$ is surjective.

\emph{Normality.} Let $\pi(h) \in \pi(G_{j-1})$ and $\pi(g) \in \pi(G_j)$, with $h \in G_{j-1}$ and $g \in G_j$. Then
\[ \pi(g)\,\pi(h)\,\pi(g)^{-1} = \pi(ghg^{-1}) \in \pi(G_{j-1}), \]
since $ghg^{-1} \in G_{j-1}$, as $G_{j-1} \trianglelefteq G_j$. So $\pi(G_{j-1}) \trianglelefteq \pi(G_j)$.

\emph{Abelian quotients.} By Abelian Quotients and Commutators (Proposition~\ref{prop:abelian-quotients-commutators}), it suffices to show that every commutator of $\pi(G_j)$ lies in $\pi(G_{j-1})$. Let $g, h \in G_j$. Since $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is abelian, the same proposition gives $ghg^{-1}h^{-1} \in G_{j-1}$. Hence
\[ \pi(g)\,\pi(h)\,\pi(g)^{-1}\pi(h)^{-1} = \pi(ghg^{-1}h^{-1}) \in \pi(G_{j-1}). \]
So $\pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1})$ is abelian, and $Q$ is solvable.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{Subquotients: An Injection and a Surjection}]\label{prop:subquotient-maps}
Let $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ be a chain, let $S \leq G$, and let $\pi: G \to Q$ be a surjective homomorphism.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $x(S \cap G_{j-1}) \mapsto xG_{j-1}$ is a well-defined injective homomorphism
    \[ (S \cap G_j)/(S \cap G_{j-1}) \hookrightarrow G_j/G_{j-1}. \]
    \item $xG_{j-1} \mapsto \pi(x)\pi(G_{j-1})$ is a well-defined surjective homomorphism
    \[ G_j/G_{j-1} \twoheadrightarrow \pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1}). \]
\end{enumerate}
So each quotient of the chain for $S$ is (isomorphic to) a \emph{subgroup} of the corresponding quotient for $G$, and each quotient of the chain for $Q$ is a \emph{quotient} (homomorphic image) of it. Since subgroups and homomorphic images of abelian groups are abelian, this proves the abelian steps of WS 9.2 and WS 9.3 once more.
\srcline{lecture 10/9}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Both maps are induced by the obvious homomorphisms out of $S \cap G_j$ and $G_j$.\par
\textbf{(1)} This is the map induced by $\alpha: S \cap G_j \to G_j/G_{j-1}$, $x \mapsto xG_{j-1}$, whose kernel is $S \cap G_{j-1}$ (proof of Subgroups of Solvable Groups Are Solvable, Proposition~\ref{prop:subgroups-solvable}). By the First Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem}) it is a well-defined isomorphism onto $\operatorname{Im}\alpha$, hence injective into $G_j/G_{j-1}$.

\textbf{(2)} Let $\varphi: G_j \to \pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1})$, $\varphi(x) = \pi(x)\pi(G_{j-1})$, the restriction of $\pi$ to $G_j$ followed by the projection; it is a surjective homomorphism, and $G_{j-1} \subseteq \operatorname{Ker}\varphi$ because $\pi(G_{j-1})$ is the identity of the target. \emph{Well defined on cosets:} if $xG_{j-1} = yG_{j-1}$, then $y = xn$ with $n \in G_{j-1}$, so $\varphi(y) = \varphi(x)\varphi(n) = \varphi(x)$. The induced map is a homomorphism because $\varphi$ is, and surjective because $\varphi$ is.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{The Trap: Do Not Cancel $N$}]\label{rem:do-not-cancel-n}
For $Q = G/N$, the isomorphism theorems describe the pieces of the chain for $Q$:
\[ \pi(G_j) \cong G_j/(G_j \cap N) \cong G_jN/N \]
(First, then Second Isomorphism Theorem, Theorems~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem} and~\ref{thm:second-isomorphism-theorem}), and so, by the Third Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:third-isomorphism-theorem}),
\[ \pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1}) \cong (G_jN/N)\big/(G_{j-1}N/N) \cong G_jN/G_{j-1}N. \]
It is tempting to ``cancel $N$'' and conclude $\pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1}) \cong G_j/G_{j-1}$. This is false in general: for $N = G$, every $\pi(G_j)$ is trivial, while $G_j/G_{j-1}$ need not be. The correct relation is the surjection of part (2) of Subquotients: An Injection and a Surjection (Proposition~\ref{prop:subquotient-maps}): $G_j/G_{j-1}$ maps \emph{onto} $\pi(G_j)/\pi(G_{j-1})$, with a kernel that can be nontrivial. The lecture's advice: rather than chase isomorphism-theorem formulas, write down the obviously well-defined injection or surjection.
\srcline{lecture 10/9}
\end{rembox}

\section{The Derived Series}\label{sec:derived-series}

\textit{Reference: not covered in the Pinter chapters used so far.}

\begin{rembox}[{The Derived Subgroup}]\label{rem:derived-subgroup}
Worksheet 9 recalls the commutator subgroup $D(G) = [G, G]$ (Definition~\ref{def:commutator-subgroup}), also called the \emph{derived subgroup}. Its normality (WS 9.4) and the commutativity of $G^{\mathrm{ab}} = G/D(G)$ (WS 9.5) were proved in \ref{sec:commutators}: Properties of the Commutator Subgroup (Proposition~\ref{prop:properties-commutator-subgroup}) and The Abelianization Is Abelian (Proposition~\ref{prop:abelianization-abelian}).
\end{rembox}

\begin{defbox}[{Derived Series}]\label{def:derived-series}
The \textbf{derived series} of $G$ is the chain $G \trianglerighteq D(G) \trianglerighteq D(D(G)) \trianglerighteq \cdots$. Its $k$-th term is written $D_k(G)$: $D_0(G) = G$ and $D_{k+1}(G) = D(D_k(G))$.
\srcline{WS 9}
\end{defbox}

\begin{thmbox}[{Solvability via the Derived Series}]\label{thm:solvable-derived-series}
$G$ is solvable if and only if $D_N(G) = \{e\}$ for some $N$.
\srcline{WS 9.6; lecture 10/9, 10/12}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} ($\Leftarrow$) the derived series has abelian quotients; ($\Rightarrow$) $D_k(G) \subseteq G_{N-k}$ by induction.\par
\textbf{($\Leftarrow$) (lecture 10/9).} Suppose $D_N(G) = \{e\}$. By Properties of the Commutator Subgroup (Proposition~\ref{prop:properties-commutator-subgroup}) and The Abelianization Is Abelian (Proposition~\ref{prop:abelianization-abelian}), applied to the group $D_k(G)$, each $D_{k+1}(G) = D(D_k(G))$ is normal in $D_k(G)$ with abelian quotient $D_k(G)/D_{k+1}(G)$. So
\[ \{e\} = D_N(G) \trianglelefteq D_{N-1}(G) \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq D_1(G) \trianglelefteq D_0(G) = G \]
is a chain as in the definition, read upwards ($G_j = D_{N-j}(G)$), and $G$ is solvable.

\textbf{($\Rightarrow$) (lecture 10/12).} Let $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ be a chain with abelian quotients. We claim that $D_k(G) \subseteq G_{N-k}$ for $k = 0, 1, \ldots, N$; the case $k = N$ gives $D_N(G) \subseteq G_0 = \{e\}$. Two facts are used:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
    \item if $H \subseteq L$ are subgroups, then $D(H) \subseteq D(L)$: every commutator of elements of $H$ is a commutator of elements of $L$, so the subgroup $D(L)$ contains all the generators of $D(H)$ (Definition~\ref{def:commutator-subgroup});
    \item if $M \trianglelefteq H$ and $H/M$ is abelian, then $D(H) \subseteq M$ (Abelian Quotients and Commutators, Proposition~\ref{prop:abelian-quotients-commutators}).
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Base case.} $D_0(G) = G = G_N$. \emph{Inductive step.} If $D_{k-1}(G) \subseteq G_{N-k+1}$, then
\[ D_k(G) = D\big(D_{k-1}(G)\big) \subseteq D(G_{N-k+1}) \subseteq G_{N-k}, \]
by fact (1) and then fact (2), applied to $G_{N-k} \trianglelefteq G_{N-k+1}$, whose quotient is abelian. (The board indexes the chain downwards, $G = G_0 \trianglerighteq G_1 \trianglerighteq \cdots \trianglerighteq G_m = \{e\}$, and proves $D_k(G) \subseteq G_k$; it is the same argument.) In words: the derived series descends at least as fast as any chain with abelian quotients.
\end{proof}

\begin{exbox}[{Derived Series of $S_3$, $S_4$ and $S_n$}]\label{ex:derived-series-sn}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $S_3 \trianglerighteq A_3 \trianglerighteq \{e\}$: $D(S_3) = A_3$ by The Commutator Subgroup and Characters of $S_n$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:commutator-subgroup-sn}), and $D(A_3) = \{e\}$ since $A_3$ is abelian.
    \item $S_4 \trianglerighteq A_4 \trianglerighteq K \trianglerighteq \{e\}$. Here $D(A_4) = K$: on one hand $A_4/K \cong \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$ is abelian, so $D(A_4) \subseteq K$ (Abelian Quotients and Commutators, Proposition~\ref{prop:abelian-quotients-commutators}); on the other hand $(1\,2\,3)(1\,2\,4)(1\,2\,3)^{-1}(1\,2\,4)^{-1} = (1\,2)(3\,4)$, and $D(A_4)$ is normal in $A_4$, so it also contains the conjugates $(1\,3)(2\,4)$ and $(1\,4)(2\,3)$ of $(1\,2)(3\,4)$ by $(1\,2\,3)$ and $(1\,3\,2)$. Finally $D(K) = \{e\}$ since $K$ is abelian.
    \item For $n \geq 5$: $D(S_n) = A_n$ and $D(A_n) = A_n$ (Characters of $A_n$ Are Trivial for $n \geq 5$, Theorem~\ref{thm:characters-an-trivial}), so the series is $S_n \trianglerighteq A_n \trianglerighteq A_n \trianglerighteq \cdots$ and never reaches $\{e\}$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{not from class}
\end{exbox}

\begin{corbox}[{$A_n$ and $S_n$ Are Not Solvable for $n \geq 5$}]\label{cor:an-sn-not-solvable}
For $n \geq 5$, neither $A_n$ nor $S_n$ is solvable. Hence $S_n$ is solvable if and only if $n \leq 4$.
\srcline{not from class}
\end{corbox}

\begin{proof}
Let $n \geq 5$ and suppose $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = A_n$ is a chain with abelian quotients. Let $j$ be the largest index with $G_j \neq A_n$. Then $G_j \trianglelefteq G_{j+1} = A_n$, and $A_n$ is simple (Theorem~\ref{thm:an-simple-n-ge-5}), so $G_j = \{e\}$; but then $G_{j+1}/G_j \cong A_n$ is not abelian, a contradiction. So $A_n$ is not solvable, and neither is $S_n$, which contains $A_n$ (Subgroups of Solvable Groups Are Solvable, Proposition~\ref{prop:subgroups-solvable}). For $n \leq 4$, $S_n$ is a subgroup of $S_4$ (fixing the remaining points), hence solvable (Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-s4-solvable}). (This argument uses simplicity of $A_n$; by WS 9.6 (Theorem~\ref{thm:solvable-derived-series}) the derived series in Example~\ref{ex:derived-series-sn} gives the same conclusion.)
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{The Derived Series as a Crank}]\label{rem:derived-series-crank}
The definition of solvability asks us to hunt for a chain, as in WS 9.1. The derived series is the mechanical alternative: turn the crank $H \mapsto D(H)$ and see whether $\{e\}$ is reached. For a finite group each step either shrinks the group or changes nothing, and once $D_{k+1}(G) = D_k(G)$ the series is stuck forever. So a finite group's series either reaches $\{e\}$ (solvable) or gets stuck at a nontrivial group $H$ with $D(H) = H$ (not solvable): $S_5 \trianglerighteq A_5 \trianglerighteq A_5 \trianglerighteq \cdots$. The lecture noted that infinite groups can instead descend forever without reaching $\{e\}$ (free groups are an example; not from class).
\srcline{lecture 10/9}
\end{rembox}
% ============================================================================
% SECTION: COMPOSITION SERIES (Lecture and Worksheet 12, Wed Oct 28)
% ============================================================================
\section{Composition Series and the Jordan--H\"older Theorem}\label{sec:composition-series}

\textit{Source: Lecture and Worksheet 12 (Wed Oct 28); the proof is to be finished Fri Oct 30. Taught after the Sylow theorems and semidirect products, and placed here because a composition series is a subnormal series, like the chains that define solvable groups (\ref{sec:solvable}) and the derived series (\ref{sec:derived-series}). Reference: not covered in the Pinter chapters used so far.}

The course has used the analogy that simple groups are atoms and other groups are molecules built from them. For the analogy to be good, every group should have a well-defined ``chemical formula'': a list of simple pieces that does not depend on how the group is taken apart. The Jordan--H\"older theorem says exactly this, for every group that can be taken apart into simple pieces at all, in particular every finite group.

\begin{defbox}[{Subnormal Series}]\label{def:subnormal-series}
Let $G$ be any group. A \textbf{subnormal series} of $G$ is a chain of subgroups
\[ \{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq G_1 \trianglelefteq G_2 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G \]
in which each $G_{j-1}$ is normal in the next term $G_j$ (not necessarily in $G$). The groups $G_j/G_{j-1}$ are its \textbf{quotients} (or subquotients).
\srcline{lecture 10/28; WS 12}
\end{defbox}

\begin{defbox}[{Composition Series}]\label{def:composition-series}
A subnormal series $\{e\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = G$ (Definition~\ref{def:subnormal-series}) is a \textbf{composition series} if every quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is simple (Definition~\ref{def:simple-group}). One then thinks of $G$ as made from $G_1/G_0, G_2/G_1, \ldots, G_N/G_{N-1}$, its \textbf{composition factors}.
\srcline{lecture 10/28; WS 12}
\end{defbox}

\begin{defbox}[{Quasi-Composition Series}]\label{def:quasi-composition-series}
A subnormal series is a \textbf{quasi-composition series} if every quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is simple or trivial.
\srcline{lecture 10/28; WS 12}
\end{defbox}

\begin{rembox}[{Quasi-Composition Series}]\label{rem:quasi-composition-cleanup}
The term is the lecturer's own and is not standard. (WS 12 says ``a composition series where \ldots''; a subnormal series is meant.) A trivial quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ just means $G_{j-1} = G_j$, a repeated term. Deleting the repeated terms turns a quasi-composition series into a composition series with the same simple quotients. Quasi-composition series are convenient in the proof below, where trivial quotients arise naturally.
\srcline{lecture 10/28}
\end{rembox}

\begin{rembox}[{Relation to Solvable Groups}]\label{rem:composition-solvable}
\textit{(Not from class.)} The chain in the definition of a solvable group (Definition~\ref{def:solvable-group}) is a subnormal series whose quotients are abelian; a composition series is one whose quotients are simple. Since the abelian simple groups are exactly the $\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:abelian-simple-groups}), a composition series all of whose factors are cyclic of prime order shows that $G$ is solvable. Conversely, a finite solvable group has such a composition series: refine each abelian quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ by a composition series of it (Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-composition-series}) and pull back, as in the proof of that lemma. Combined with the Jordan--H\"older theorem: \emph{a finite group is solvable if and only if all of its composition factors are cyclic of prime order.} For example, $\{e\} \trianglelefteq \langle (1\,2)(3\,4) \rangle \trianglelefteq K \trianglelefteq A_4 \trianglelefteq S_4$, with $K$ the Klein four-group, has factors $\mathbb{Z}/2, \mathbb{Z}/2, \mathbb{Z}/3, \mathbb{Z}/2$, while $A_5$ has the single factor $A_5$.
\end{rembox}

\begin{thmbox}[{Jordan--H\"older Theorem}]\label{thm:jordan-holder}
Let $G$ be any group, and let
\[ \{e\} = X_0 \trianglelefteq X_1 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq X_m = G \quad\text{and}\quad \{e\} = Y_0 \trianglelefteq Y_1 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq Y_n = G \]
be two composition series, with quotients $S_i = X_i/X_{i-1}$ and $T_j = Y_j/Y_{j-1}$. Then $m = n$, and there is a permutation $\sigma \in S_n$ with $S_i \cong T_{\sigma(i)}$ for $1 \leq i \leq m$.
\srcline{lecture 10/28; WS 12}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}[Proof (to be filled)]
The proof occupies \ref{sub:jh-grid}: the steps through WS 12.3 were done on 10/28, and WS 12.4--12.6 are to be finished on Fri Oct 30. The lecture's comment: the theorem justifies the program of studying groups through simple groups.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{$\mathbb{Z}$ Has No Composition Series}]\label{prop:z-no-composition-series}
Not every group has a composition series: $\mathbb{Z}$ has none.
\srcline{lecture 10/28}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} the factors of a composition series of an abelian group are finite, so the group would be finite.\par
\textit{(Lecture 10/28, answering a student's question.)} Suppose $\{0\} = G_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq G_N = \mathbb{Z}$ were a composition series. Every quotient $G_j/G_{j-1}$ is a quotient of a subgroup of an abelian group, hence abelian, and simple, hence $\cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ for a prime $p$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:abelian-simple-groups}). Then $|G_j| = |G_{j-1}| \cdot |G_j/G_{j-1}|$ (Lagrange, Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}) shows by induction that every $G_j$ is finite, including $G_N = \mathbb{Z}$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lembox}[{Finite Groups Have Composition Series}]\label{lem:finite-composition-series}
Every finite group $G$ has a composition series.
\srcline{lecture 10/28}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} split $G$ at a proper nontrivial normal subgroup $N$ and stitch a series of $N$ to the preimage of a series of $G/N$.\par
\textit{(Board 10/28.)} Strong induction on $|G|$. If $G$ is trivial, $\{e\} = G_0 = G$ is a composition series (with no quotients); if $G$ is simple, $\{e\} \trianglelefteq G$ is one. Otherwise there is a normal subgroup $N$ with $\{e\} \subsetneq N \subsetneq G$. Then $|N| < |G|$ and $|G/N| < |G|$, so by induction there are composition series
\[ \{e\} = N_0 \trianglelefteq N_1 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq N_a = N, \qquad \{e\} = Q_0 \trianglelefteq Q_1 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq Q_b = G/N. \]
Let $\pi: G \to G/N$ be the projection. The lecture's image: $N$ is an island in the middle of the stream, where the pylons of the bridge from $\{e\}$ to $G$ can be planted. Stitch the two series together:
\[ \{e\} = N_0 \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq N_a = N = \pi^{-1}(Q_0) \trianglelefteq \pi^{-1}(Q_1) \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq \pi^{-1}(Q_b) = G. \]
The first half has simple quotients by assumption. For the second half, consider $\alpha_j: \pi^{-1}(Q_j) \to Q_j/Q_{j-1}$, $x \mapsto \pi(x)Q_{j-1}$. It is a homomorphism, it is surjective because $\pi$ maps $\pi^{-1}(Q_j)$ onto $Q_j$, and its kernel is $\{x : \pi(x) \in Q_{j-1}\} = \pi^{-1}(Q_{j-1})$. So $\pi^{-1}(Q_{j-1})$ is normal in $\pi^{-1}(Q_j)$ (Kernels Are Normal, Proposition~\ref{prop:kernels-normal}), and by the First Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem})
\[ \pi^{-1}(Q_j)/\pi^{-1}(Q_{j-1}) \cong Q_j/Q_{j-1}, \]
which is simple. (The board cites the Third Isomorphism Theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:third-isomorphism-theorem}, or the correspondence theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:correspondence}, for this isomorphism; the argument above gives it directly.) Hence the stitched chain is a composition series of $G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small, >=stealth, x=1.15cm]

\textit{(Not from lecture; figure added, after the lecture's image.)} The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-composition-series}: the normal subgroup $N$ is the island in the stream from $\{e\}$ to $G$. A composition series of $N$ reaches the island, and the preimages $\pi^{-1}(Q_j)$ of a composition series of $G/N$ carry the bridge on to $G$; each new quotient is $\cong Q_j/Q_{j-1}$.

\begin{exbox}[{The Order of the Factors Is Not Arbitrary}]\label{ex:s3-composition-order}
A student asked whether every ordering of the composition factors is realized by some composition series. The question was left open in class; the answer is no. $S_3$ has the composition series $\{e\} \trianglelefteq A_3 \trianglelefteq S_3$, with factors $\mathbb{Z}/3, \mathbb{Z}/2$ in that order, and no composition series with the factors in the order $\mathbb{Z}/2, \mathbb{Z}/3$.
\srcline{question from class 10/28; answer not from class}
\end{exbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{(Not from class.)} A composition series with first factor $\mathbb{Z}/2$ and second $\mathbb{Z}/3$ would be $\{e\} \trianglelefteq G_1 \trianglelefteq S_3$ with $|G_1| = 2$ and $G_1$ normal in $S_3$. But the subgroups of order $2$ of $S_3$ are the $\langle (i\,j) \rangle$, and none is normal: $(1\,3)(1\,2)(1\,3) = (2\,3)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lembox}[{Two Normal Subgroups with Simple Quotients}]\label{lem:two-simple-quotients}
Let $G$ be a finite group and $N_1 \neq N_2$ normal subgroups such that $G/N_1$ and $G/N_2$ are both simple. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $N_1 N_2 = G$;
    \item $G/N_1 \cong N_2/(N_1 \cap N_2)$ and $G/N_2 \cong N_1/(N_1 \cap N_2)$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{PS 7.4; ``I expect to use this lemma in Friday's class''}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $N_1N_2/N_1$ is a normal subgroup of the simple group $G/N_1$, and likewise for $N_2$; they cannot both be trivial since $N_1 \neq N_2$, so $N_1N_2 = G$, and the Second Isomorphism Theorem gives the quotients.\par
\textit{(Problem Set 7, Problem 7.4, as submitted.)} \textbf{(1)} \textbf{$N_1N_2 = N_2N_1$.} Let $n_1 \in N_1$ and $n_2 \in N_2$. Since $N_1$ is normal, $n_2n_1n_2^{-1} \in N_1$, so
\[
n_2n_1 = (n_2n_1n_2^{-1})\,n_2 \in N_1N_2.
\]
Hence $N_2N_1 \subseteq N_1N_2$. Likewise, since $N_2$ is normal, $n_1n_2 = n_2\,(n_2^{-1}n_1n_2) \in N_2N_1$, so $N_1N_2 \subseteq N_2N_1$. Therefore $N_1N_2 = N_2N_1$.

\textbf{$N_1N_2$ is a subgroup of $G$.} It contains $e = e \cdot e$. For $a_1, a_2 \in N_1$ and $b_1, b_2 \in N_2$, we have $b_1a_2 \in N_2N_1 = N_1N_2$, say $b_1a_2 = a'b'$ with $a' \in N_1$, $b' \in N_2$, so
\[
(a_1b_1)(a_2b_2) = a_1(b_1a_2)b_2 = (a_1a')(b'b_2) \in N_1N_2,
\]
and $(a_1b_1)^{-1} = b_1^{-1}a_1^{-1} \in N_2N_1 = N_1N_2$.

\textbf{$N_1N_2$ is normal in $G$.} For $g \in G$, since $N_1$ and $N_2$ are normal,
\[
gN_1N_2g^{-1} = (gN_1g^{-1})(gN_2g^{-1}) = N_1N_2.
\]

\textbf{The quotients.} Since $N_1 \subseteq N_1N_2$ and $N_1$ is normal in $G$, hence in $N_1N_2$, the quotient $N_1N_2/N_1 = \{xN_1 : x \in N_1N_2\}$ is a subgroup of $G/N_1$. It is normal in $G/N_1$: for $gN_1 \in G/N_1$ and $x \in N_1N_2$,
\[
(gN_1)(xN_1)(gN_1)^{-1} = gxg^{-1}N_1 \in N_1N_2/N_1,
\]
since $gxg^{-1} \in N_1N_2$. Since $G/N_1$ is simple, $N_1N_2/N_1$ is either trivial or all of $G/N_1$. In the same way, $N_1N_2/N_2$ is a normal subgroup of $G/N_2$, so it is either trivial or all of $G/N_2$.

\textbf{They cannot both be trivial.} Suppose $N_1N_2/N_1$ is trivial, that is, $xN_1 = N_1$ for every $x \in N_1N_2$. Since $xN_1 = N_1$ if and only if $x \in N_1$, this gives $N_1N_2 \subseteq N_1$; conversely $N_1 \subseteq N_1N_2$, as $n_1 = n_1e$. So $N_1N_2 = N_1$, and hence $N_2 \subseteq N_1N_2 = N_1$, as $n_2 = en_2$. In the same way, if $N_1N_2/N_2$ is trivial, then $N_1 \subseteq N_2$. If both were trivial, we would have $N_1 = N_2$, contradicting $N_1 \neq N_2$.

\textbf{Conclusion.} Hence at least one of $N_1N_2/N_1 = G/N_1$ and $N_1N_2/N_2 = G/N_2$ holds. Since the hypotheses are symmetric in $N_1$ and $N_2$ and $N_1N_2 = N_2N_1$, we may assume without loss of generality that $N_1N_2/N_1 = G/N_1$. By Lagrange's theorem,
\[
\frac{|N_1N_2|}{|N_1|} = |N_1N_2/N_1| = |G/N_1| = \frac{|G|}{|N_1|},
\]
so $|N_1N_2| = |G|$. Since $N_1N_2$ is a subset of the finite group $G$, $N_1N_2 = G$.

\textbf{(2)} Recall the second isomorphism theorem: if $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ and $N$ is a normal subgroup of $G$, then $HN$ is a subgroup of $G$, $H \cap N$ is normal in $H$, and
\[
H/(H \cap N) \cong HN/N.
\]

\textbf{$G/N_2 \cong N_1/(N_1 \cap N_2)$.} Take $H = N_1$ and $N = N_2$, which is normal in $G$. The second isomorphism theorem gives
\[
N_1/(N_1 \cap N_2) \cong N_1N_2/N_2 = G/N_2,
\]
using $N_1N_2 = G$ from (1).

\textbf{$G/N_1 \cong N_2/(N_1 \cap N_2)$.} Take $H = N_2$ and $N = N_1$, which is normal in $G$. The second isomorphism theorem gives
\[
N_2/(N_2 \cap N_1) \cong N_2N_1/N_1 = G/N_1,
\]
using $N_2N_1 = N_1N_2 = G$ from (1). Since $N_2 \cap N_1 = N_1 \cap N_2$, this is $G/N_1 \cong N_2/(N_1 \cap N_2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{The Quotient by $N_1 \cap N_2$}]\label{rem:two-simple-quotients-product}
Speyer, on Problem 7.4: in this situation one can further show that $G/(N_1 \cap N_2) \cong N_1/(N_1 \cap N_2) \times N_2/(N_1 \cap N_2)$. \textit{(Proof not from class.)} In $\bar G = G/(N_1 \cap N_2)$, the images $\bar N_i = N_i/(N_1 \cap N_2)$ are normal, they intersect trivially, since $\bar N_1 \cap \bar N_2 = (N_1 \cap N_2)/(N_1 \cap N_2)$, and $\bar N_1 \bar N_2 = \bar G$ because $N_1 N_2 = G$. So $\bar G \cong \bar N_1 \times \bar N_2$ by Internal Direct Products Are Direct Products (Theorem~\ref{thm:internal-direct-product}, in Chapter~10).
\end{rembox}

\subsection{The Grid of Intersections}\label{sub:jh-grid}

\textit{The approach is taken from Ravi Vakil's \emph{Foundations of Algebraic Geometry} (WS 12). Throughout, the two composition series are those of the Jordan--H\"older theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:jordan-holder}).}

\begin{defbox}[{The Grid $Z_{ij}$}]\label{def:jh-grid}
Put
\[ Z_{ij} = X_i \cap Y_j \qquad (0 \leq i \leq m,\ 0 \leq j \leq n), \]
arranged in a rectangular grid as on the worksheet: $i$ increasing to the right (east) and $j$ increasing upwards (north), so $Z_{00}$ is the bottom left corner and $Z_{mn} = G$ the top right.
\srcline{WS 12; lecture 10/28}
\end{defbox}

\begin{lembox}[{Intersections in the Grid}]\label{lem:jh-grid-intersections}
$Z_{ab} \cap Z_{cd} = Z_{\min(a,c)\,\min(b,d)}$. In particular $Z_{i0} = Z_{0j} = \{e\}$, $Z_{in} = X_i$ and $Z_{mj} = Y_j$.
\srcline{WS 12; lecture 10/28}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
Both series increase, so $X_a \cap X_c = X_{\min(a,c)}$ and $Y_b \cap Y_d = Y_{\min(b,d)}$; hence $Z_{ab} \cap Z_{cd} = (X_a \cap X_c) \cap (Y_b \cap Y_d) = X_{\min(a,c)} \cap Y_{\min(b,d)}$. For the special cases, $X_i \cap \{e\} = \{e\} \cap Y_j = \{e\}$, $X_i \cap G = X_i$, $G \cap Y_j = Y_j$.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{Normality in the Grid}]\label{prop:jh-grid-normality}
$Z_{(i-1)j} \trianglelefteq Z_{ij}$ and $Z_{i(j-1)} \trianglelefteq Z_{ij}$.
\srcline{WS 12.2}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} conjugating by $z \in X_i \cap Y_j$ preserves $X_{i-1}$ (normal in $X_i$) and $Y_j$ (a group containing $z$).\par
\textit{(Worksheet session 10/28.)} Let $z \in Z_{ij} = X_i \cap Y_j$ and $w \in Z_{(i-1)j} = X_{i-1} \cap Y_j$. Then $zwz^{-1} \in X_{i-1}$, because $X_{i-1} \trianglelefteq X_i$ and $z \in X_i$; and $zwz^{-1} \in Y_j$, because $z, w \in Y_j$. So $zwz^{-1} \in Z_{(i-1)j}$. The second statement is symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{defbox}[{Edge Quotients $H_{ij}$ and $V_{ij}$}]\label{def:jh-edge-quotients}
For $i, j \geq 1$ (and $H_{i0}$, $V_{0j}$ likewise) define, using Proposition~\ref{prop:jh-grid-normality},
\[ H_{ij} = Z_{ij}/Z_{(i-1)j} \quad \text{(horizontal edges)}, \qquad V_{ij} = Z_{ij}/Z_{i(j-1)} \quad \text{(vertical edges)}. \]
A trivial $H_{ij}$ or $V_{ij}$ means that the two groups at the ends of the edge are equal; the worksheet labels such an edge ``$=$''.
\srcline{WS 12; lecture 10/28}
\end{defbox}

%% FIGURE (worksheet): the grid, redrawn
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=2.2cm, y=1.3cm, font=\small, >=stealth]
  \foreach \i/\li in {0/0, 1/1, 2/2, 3/m} {
    \foreach \j/\lj in {0/0, 1/1, 2/2, 3/n} {
      \node (z\i\j) at (\i, \j) {$Z_{\li\lj}$};
    }
  }
  \foreach \j in {0,1,2,3} { \draw[->] (z0\j) -- (z1\j); \draw[->] (z1\j) -- (z2\j); \draw[->, dashed] (z2\j) -- (z3\j); }
  \foreach \i in {0,1,2,3} { \draw[->] (z\i0) -- (z\i1); \draw[->] (z\i1) -- (z\i2); \draw[->, dashed] (z\i2) -- (z\i3); }
  \node[above, font=\scriptsize, blue!60!black] at (1.5, 1.02) {$H_{21}$};
  \node[right, font=\scriptsize, red!60!black] at (2.03, 1.5) {$V_{22}$};
  \node[font=\scriptsize, anchor=west, align=left] at (3.35, 3) {top row: $Z_{in} = X_i$};
  \node[font=\scriptsize, anchor=west, align=left] at (3.35, 1.5) {right column:\\ $Z_{mj} = Y_j$};
  \node[font=\scriptsize, anchor=west, align=left] at (3.35, 0) {bottom row, left column: $\{e\}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\textit{(Figure from WS 12, redrawn.)} The grid $Z_{ij} = X_i \cap Y_j$. Arrows are inclusions, each normal (Proposition~\ref{prop:jh-grid-normality}). The bottom row and left column are trivial; the top row is the $X$-series ($Z_{in} = X_i$) and the right column the $Y$-series ($Z_{mj} = Y_j$). Horizontal edges carry the quotients $H_{ij}$, vertical edges the $V_{ij}$.

\begin{rembox}[{The Strategy}]\label{rem:jh-strategy}
Every path through the grid from $Z_{00}$ to $Z_{mn}$, travelling one step east or north at a time, is a chain of subgroups. The essence of the proof is to show that every such path is a quasi-composition series, with the same simple quotients in each case. The two extreme paths give the $S_i$ and the $T_j$ (WS 12.1).
\srcline{WS 12}
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{The Two Edge Paths}]\label{prop:jh-edge-paths}
$(Z_{00}, Z_{10}, \ldots, Z_{m0}, Z_{m1}, \ldots, Z_{mn})$ and $(Z_{00}, Z_{01}, \ldots, Z_{0n}, Z_{1n}, \ldots, Z_{mn})$ are quasi-composition series. The quotients of the first are $m$ trivial groups followed by $T_1, \ldots, T_n$; those of the second are $n$ trivial groups followed by $S_1, \ldots, S_m$.
\srcline{WS 12.1}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} the bottom row and left column are trivial, the right column and top row are the two series.\par
\textit{(Worksheet session 10/28.)} By Lemma~\ref{lem:jh-grid-intersections}, $Z_{i0} = \{e\}$ and $Z_{mj} = Y_j$, so the first path is $\{e\}, \ldots, \{e\}$ ($m + 1$ times) followed by $Y_1, \ldots, Y_n$: its quotients are trivial $m$ times, then $Y_j/Y_{j-1} = T_j$, each simple. The second is symmetric, with $Z_{0j} = \{e\}$ and $Z_{in} = X_i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{Horizontal Edges Propagate}]\label{prop:jh-horizontal-edges}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The inclusion $Z_{i(j-1)} \hookrightarrow Z_{ij}$ induces a well-defined injection $H_{i(j-1)} \hookrightarrow H_{ij}$.
    \item The image of $H_{i(j-1)}$ is normal in $H_{ij}$.
    \item Each $H_{ij}$ is trivial or isomorphic to $S_i$. More precisely, in each column $i$ there is $j_0$ with $H_{ij}$ trivial for $j < j_0$ and $H_{ij} \cong S_i$ for $j \geq j_0$.
\end{enumerate}
Similarly, each $V_{ij}$ is trivial or isomorphic to $T_j$.
\srcline{WS 12.3; board 10/28}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} restricting $Z_{ij} \to H_{ij}$ to $Z_{i(j-1)}$ has kernel $Z_{(i-1)(j-1)}$; so the column $H_{i0} \hookrightarrow \cdots \hookrightarrow H_{in} = S_i$ is a chain of normal subgroups ending in a simple group.\par
\textit{(Board 10/28.)} \textbf{(1), (2).} Let $\pi: Z_{ij} \to H_{ij} = Z_{ij}/Z_{(i-1)j}$ be the projection, and restrict it to $Z_{i(j-1)}$. By Restricting a Homomorphism (Lemma~\ref{lem:restricting-homomorphism}), the restriction has kernel
\[ Z_{(i-1)j} \cap Z_{i(j-1)} = Z_{(i-1)(j-1)} \]
(Lemma~\ref{lem:jh-grid-intersections}), so by the First Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:first-isomorphism-theorem}) $\pi$ induces an injective homomorphism
\[ H_{i(j-1)} = Z_{i(j-1)}/Z_{(i-1)(j-1)} \;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\; \pi(Z_{i(j-1)}) \;\subseteq\; H_{ij}, \qquad x Z_{(i-1)(j-1)} \mapsto x Z_{(i-1)j}. \]
Since $Z_{i(j-1)} \trianglelefteq Z_{ij}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:jh-grid-normality}) and $\pi$ is surjective, the image $\pi(Z_{i(j-1)})$ is normal in $H_{ij}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:preimages-images-normal-subgroups}, WS 6.6(3)).

This is part (1) of Proposition~\ref{prop:subquotient-maps}, applied in the group $Y_j$ to the chain $Z_{0j} \trianglelefteq \cdots \trianglelefteq Z_{mj} = Y_j$ and the subgroup $Y_{j-1}$, whose intersections with the chain are the $Z_{i(j-1)}$. The new ingredient is the normality in (2).

\textbf{(3)} By (1) and (2), each $H_{i(j-1)}$ is isomorphic to a normal subgroup of $H_{ij}$, giving a chain up the column
\[ \{e\} = H_{i0} \hookrightarrow H_{i1} \hookrightarrow \cdots \hookrightarrow H_{in} = S_i, \]
where $H_{i0} = Z_{i0}/Z_{(i-1)0} = \{e\}/\{e\}$ and $H_{in} = X_i/X_{i-1} = S_i$. Work down from the top of the column. $S_i$ is simple, so its normal subgroup $\pi(Z_{i(n-1)})$ is trivial or all of $S_i$; hence $H_{i(n-1)}$ is trivial or $\cong S_i$. If $H_{ij}$ is trivial or $\cong S_i$, the same argument applies to $H_{i(j-1)}$, a normal subgroup of $H_{ij}$ up to isomorphism. So every $H_{ij}$ is trivial or $\cong S_i$. Since $H_{i(j-1)}$ injects into $H_{ij}$, once some $H_{ij}$ is nontrivial so are all those above it in the column, which gives $j_0$. (The induction works one step at a time; it does not use transitivity of normality, which fails.) The statement for $V_{ij}$ is the same argument with the roles of $X$ and $Y$ exchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{The Possible Squares}]\label{rem:jh-squares}
Consider one square of the grid, with corners $Z_{(i-1)(j-1)}$ (bottom left), $Z_{i(j-1)}$ (bottom right), $Z_{(i-1)j}$ (top left), $Z_{ij}$ (top right). Its bottom and top edges carry $H_{i(j-1)}$ and $H_{ij}$, its left and right edges $V_{(i-1)j}$ and $V_{ij}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:jh-horizontal-edges}, each edge is ``$=$'' (trivial) or carries $S_i$ (horizontal) or $T_j$ (vertical), and a nontrivial bottom edge forces a nontrivial top edge, a nontrivial left edge a nontrivial right edge. This leaves $3 \times 3 = 9$ kinds of square.
\srcline{WS 12.4--12.5}
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{Four Impossible Squares}]\label{prop:jh-impossible-squares}
The following squares do not occur (top, bottom; left, right):
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
    \item $S_i$, $=$; \ $T_j$, $T_j$;
    \item $S_i$, $S_i$; \ $=$, $T_j$;
    \item $S_i$, $=$; \ $=$, $=$;
    \item $=$, $=$; \ $=$, $T_j$.
\end{enumerate}
Hint: think about $Z_{ij}/Z_{(i-1)(j-1)}$.
\srcline{WS 12.4}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}[Proof (to be filled)]
WS 12.4, to be done Fri Oct 30.
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{The Crossing Square}]\label{prop:jh-crossing-square}
If a square has top $S_i$, bottom $=$, left $=$, right $T_j$, then $S_i \cong T_j$. Hint: think about $Z_{ij}/Z_{(i-1)(j-1)}$.
\srcline{WS 12.5}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}[Proof (to be filled)]
WS 12.5, to be done Fri Oct 30.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{Pipes}]\label{rem:jh-pipes}
So only five squares remain: both pairs nontrivial; only the left and right edges nontrivial; only the top and bottom edges nontrivial; all four edges trivial; and the crossing square of WS 12.5. Draw a ``pipe'' through each nontrivial pair of opposite edges, and in the crossing square a pipe entering through the top edge and leaving through the right edge. Each pipe then carries a single simple group from the top row ($S_i$) to the right column ($T_j$), which is how the permutation $\sigma$ arises. WS 12.6 asks to turn this into a proof of the Jordan--H\"older theorem; it is to be finished on Fri Oct 30.
\srcline{WS 12}
\end{rembox}
