\section{\texorpdfstring{$S_3$, $S_4$, $A_n$ and $GL_n$}{S₃, S₄, Aₙ and GLₙ}}\label{sec:rev-ch9}

\textit{The recurring groups as the first part of Chapter~9 saw them: characters take values in abelian groups, so they cannot separate conjugates and they kill commutators; and the solvable groups are those built from abelian pieces; composition series break them into simple pieces, the composition factors. Each part gathers the chapter's items about one group, in order.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Symmetric Group $S_3$}{The Symmetric Group S₃}}\label{sec:rev-ch9-s3}

$S_3$ shows why a character needs an abelian target: the identity map $S_3 \to S_3$ separates the conjugate transpositions $(1\,2)$ and $(1\,3)$.

The remark \emph{Where Commutativity of $A$ Is Needed} (\ref{sec:characters}) gives the example.

$S_3$ is solvable without being abelian: the chain $\{e\} \trianglelefteq A_3 \trianglelefteq S_3$ has the abelian quotients $\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$ and $\{\pm 1\}$, and the derived series $S_3 \trianglerighteq A_3 \trianglerighteq \{e\}$ reaches $\{e\}$ in two steps.

Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-s4-solvable} (WS 9.1) gives the chain; Example~\ref{ex:derived-series-sn}(1) computes the derived series.

The same chain is the only composition series of $S_3$ ($A_3$ is its only proper nontrivial normal subgroup), with factors $\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$ in that order; the other order does not occur (Example~\ref{ex:s3-composition-order}).

\textit{$S_3$ elsewhere: previous, Chapter~8 (\ref{sec:rev-ch8-s3}); no later appearance yet.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$S_4$, $A_4$ and the Klein Four-Group $K$}{S₄, A₄ and the Klein Four-Group K}}\label{sec:rev-ch9-s4}

$S_4$ is solvable through $\{e\} \trianglelefteq K \trianglelefteq A_4 \trianglelefteq S_4$, with quotients $K$, $\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$ and $\{\pm 1\}$, and this chain is its derived series: $D(S_4) = A_4$, $D(A_4) = K$, $D(K) = \{e\}$. Here $K$ is normal in all of $S_4$, more than the definition asks (Remark~\ref{rem:normal-in-next-term}).

Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-s4-solvable} (WS 9.1) gives the chain; Example~\ref{ex:derived-series-sn}(2) computes the derived series, finding $(1\,2)(3\,4)$ as a commutator of two $3$-cycles of $A_4$.

Refining $\{e\} \trianglelefteq K$ by $\langle (1\,2)(3\,4) \rangle$ gives a composition series of $S_4$, with factors $\mathbb{Z}/2, \mathbb{Z}/2, \mathbb{Z}/3, \mathbb{Z}/2$ (the remark \emph{Relation to Solvable Groups} in \ref{sec:composition-series}); by the Jordan--H\"older theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:jordan-holder}) every composition series of $S_4$ has these factors.

$A_4$ also appears in the $A_n$ part below, as the case $n = 4$ of the commutator computations.

\textit{$S_4$, $A_4$ and $K$ elsewhere: previous, Chapter~8 (\ref{sec:rev-ch8-s4}); no later appearance yet.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Alternating Groups $A_n$}{The Alternating Groups Aₙ}}\label{sec:rev-ch9-an}

$A_n$ is the commutator subgroup of $S_n$: every $3$-cycle is a commutator and no transposition is. Hence every character of $S_n$ is trivial on $A_n$, and the abelianization of $S_n$ is $S_n/A_n \cong \{\pm 1\}$. For $n \geq 5$, $A_n$ is also its own commutator subgroup, so its characters are trivial; $A_3$ and $A_4$ show that the bound is sharp.

Propositions~\ref{prop:3-cycles-commutators} and~\ref{prop:transpositions-not-commutators} (PS~2.4) separate $3$-cycles from transpositions; Theorem~\ref{thm:commutator-subgroup-sn} (PS~4.5) proves $[S_n, S_n] = A_n$; Example~\ref{ex:abelianization-sn} computes the abelianization; Lemma~\ref{lem:3-cycles-commutators-an} and Theorem~\ref{thm:characters-an-trivial} (PS~5.3) prove $[A_n, A_n] = A_n$ for $n \geq 5$; the remark \emph{Small $n$, and the Link to Simplicity} (\ref{sec:commutators}) treats $A_3$ and $A_4$ and compares with the simplicity of $A_n$.

For $n \geq 5$, $A_n$ is not solvable, and hence neither is $S_n$: a simple non-abelian group has no chain with abelian quotients, and the derived series of $S_n$ stalls at $A_n = D(A_n)$. So $S_n$ is solvable exactly for $n \leq 4$. For $n \geq 5$, $\{e\} \trianglelefteq A_n \trianglelefteq S_n$ is a composition series, with factors $A_n$ and $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$; the factor $A_n$ is not cyclic of prime order, as non-solvability requires (the remark \emph{Relation to Solvable Groups} in \ref{sec:composition-series}).

Example~\ref{ex:derived-series-sn}(3) shows the derived series stalling; Corollary~\ref{cor:an-sn-not-solvable} proves non-solvability from the simplicity of $A_n$ and Proposition~\ref{prop:subgroups-solvable}.

\textit{$A_n$ elsewhere: previous, Chapter~8 (\ref{sec:rev-ch8-an}); no later appearance yet.}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$GL_n$, $SL_n$ and $O(n)$}{GLₙ, SLₙ and O(n)}}\label{sec:rev-ch9-gl}

The determinant is the model character of $GL_n(k)$, and a character is the same thing as a $1$-dimensional representation, a homomorphism into $GL_1(k) = k^\times$.

Example~\ref{ex:characters}(2) lists $\det$ among the first characters; the remark \emph{One-Dimensional Representations} (\ref{sec:characters}) identifies characters with homomorphisms into $GL_1(k)$; Example~\ref{ex:products-commutators-not-commutators} (PS~5.4) finds, in a group of real block upper-triangular matrices, elements of $[G, G]$ that are not commutators.

\textit{$GL_n$ elsewhere: previous, Chapter~8 (\ref{sec:rev-ch8-gl}); next, the Sylow part of this chapter, where $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ appears once (Proposition~\ref{prop:unitriangular-sylow}).}

\section{\texorpdfstring{$p$-Groups and Sylow Subgroups}{p-Groups and Sylow Subgroups}}\label{sec:p-groups}

\textit{Reference: Worksheet 10. Throughout, $p$ is a prime.}

\begin{rembox}[{The Question}]\label{rem:sylow-question}
By Lagrange (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}), if $H \leq G$ and $G$ is finite, then $|H|$ divides $|G|$. Conversely, if $m$ divides $|G|$, is there a subgroup of order $m$? Not in general: $A_4$ has order $12$ but no subgroup of order $6$ (The Converse of Lagrange Fails: $A_4$, Example~\ref{ex:converse-lagrange-fails-a4}). The lecture's answer is yes when $m$ is a power of a prime. The case of a prime $m = p$ is Cauchy's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:cauchys-theorem}), and the case of the largest power of $p$ dividing $|G|$ is the First Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-first}). For example, $|S_3| = 6$, and $\langle (1\,2) \rangle$ and $\langle (1\,2\,3) \rangle$ have orders $2$ and $3$. The lecture also asked for a subgroup of order $8$ in $S_4$, of order $24$ (Example~\ref{ex:sylow-s3-s4} below).
\srcline{lecture 10/12}
\end{rembox}

Worksheet 10 begins by restating the definition of a $p$-group, already met with the center:

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Recall: Definition~\ref{def:p-group} ($p$-Group).} For a prime $p$, a \textbf{$p$-group} is a group of order $p^k$ for some $k \geq 0$.
\end{quote}

Nontrivial $p$-groups have nontrivial center (Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-power-order-have-nontrivial-center}, PS 3.5); for groups of order $p^2$ this already decides the group (\ref{sub:order-p-squared}, PS 6.3).

\begin{defbox}[{$p$-Subgroup}]\label{def:p-subgroup}
For a group $G$, a \textbf{$p$-subgroup} of $G$ is a subgroup of $G$ which is a $p$-group.
\srcline{WS 10}
\end{defbox}

\begin{propbox}[{Fixed Points of a $p$-Group Action}]\label{prop:p-group-fixed-point}
Let $P$ be a $p$-group and let $X$ be a finite set on which $P$ acts. If $|X| \not\equiv 0 \pmod p$, then $P$ fixes some point of $X$.
\srcline{WS 10.1}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Orbits of size $> 1$ have size divisible by $p$, so $|X| \equiv |X^P| \pmod p$.\par
\textit{(Not from lecture; filled in. The route is the counting the lecture of 10/16 used in the proof of the Third Sylow Theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}: the orbits of a $p$-group have size a power of $p$, so $|X| \equiv |X^P| \pmod p$.)} Write $|P| = p^k$ (Definition~\ref{def:p-group}), write $\star$ for the action (Definition~\ref{def:action-right-action}), and let
\[ X^P = \{ x \in X : g \star x = x \text{ for all } g \in P \} = \bigcap_{g \in P} \operatorname{Fix}(g) \]
be the set of points fixed by every element of $P$ (Definition~\ref{def:fixed-points}). We must show $X^P \neq \varnothing$. We show that $|X^P| \equiv |X| \pmod p$.

\textbf{(1) $X$ is the disjoint union of its orbits.} By Orbits Partition $X$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:orbits-partition-x}), $X$ is the disjoint union of the distinct orbits $Px = \{ g \star x : g \in P \}$ (Definition~\ref{def:orbit-orbit-space}). Since $X$ is finite there are finitely many, say $O_1, \ldots, O_r$, and
\[ |X| = |O_1| + |O_2| + \cdots + |O_r|. \]

\textbf{(2) Every orbit has size a power of $p$.} Let $x \in X$. By Orbit--Stabilizer (Theorem~\ref{thm:orbit-stabilizer}), with $\operatorname{Stab}(x)$ the stabilizer (Definition~\ref{def:stabilizer-fixed-points}),
\[ p^k = |P| = |Px| \cdot |\operatorname{Stab}(x)|, \]
so $|Px|$ is a positive divisor of $p^k$. If $|Px| > 1$, writing $|Px|$ and $|\operatorname{Stab}(x)|$ as products of primes gives a prime factorization of $p^k$, which by Unique Prime Factorization (Theorem~\ref{thm:unique-prime-factorization}) consists of $k$ copies of $p$; so every prime factor of $|Px|$ is $p$. Hence $|Px| = p^\ell$ for some $\ell$ with $0 \leq \ell \leq k$.

\textbf{(3) The orbits of size $1$ are the fixed points.} Since $x = e \star x \in Px$,
\[ |Px| = 1 \iff Px = \{x\} \iff g \star x = x \text{ for all } g \in P \iff x \in X^P. \]
So the orbits of size $1$ are exactly the singletons $\{x\}$ with $x \in X^P$, and there are $|X^P|$ of them. By (2), every other orbit has size $p^\ell$ with $\ell \geq 1$, which is divisible by $p$.

\textbf{(4) Counting modulo $p$.} Split the sum in (1) into the orbits of size $1$ and the others, writing $|O_i| = p^{\ell_i}$ with $\ell_i \geq 1$ for the latter (by (3)):
\[ |X| = \sum_{x \in X^P} 1 + \sum_{i \,:\, |O_i| > 1} p^{\ell_i} = |X^P| + p \sum_{i \,:\, |O_i| > 1} p^{\ell_i - 1} \equiv |X^P| \pmod p. \]
By hypothesis $|X| \not\equiv 0 \pmod p$, so $|X^P| \not\equiv 0 \pmod p$; in particular $|X^P| \neq 0$. So there is a point $x \in X^P$, that is, a point of $X$ fixed by every element of $P$.
\end{proof}

Let $G$ be a finite group, and factor $|G| = p^k m$ where $p$ does not divide $m$.

\begin{defbox}[{Sylow $p$-Subgroup}]\label{def:sylow-subgroup}
A \textbf{Sylow $p$-subgroup} of $G$ is a subgroup of $G$ of order $p^k$.
\srcline{WS 10}
\end{defbox}

\begin{exbox}[{Sylow Subgroups of $S_3$ and $S_4$}]\label{ex:sylow-s3-s4}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $|S_3| = 6 = 2 \cdot 3$. The Sylow $2$-subgroups are $\langle (1\,2) \rangle$, $\langle (1\,3) \rangle$, $\langle (2\,3) \rangle$, and the only Sylow $3$-subgroup is $A_3 = \langle (1\,2\,3) \rangle$.
    \item $|S_4| = 24 = 2^3 \cdot 3$. The Sylow $2$-subgroups have order $8$: they are the three subgroups $K \cup \tau K$ ($\tau$ a transposition) containing $K$, found in The Correspondence for $S_4$ and $K$ (Example~\ref{ex:correspondence-s4-v}). The Sylow $3$-subgroups are the four subgroups $\langle (1\,2\,3) \rangle$, $\langle (1\,2\,4) \rangle$, $\langle (1\,3\,4) \rangle$, $\langle (2\,3\,4) \rangle$.
\end{enumerate}
The numbers of Sylow subgroups are $3, 1$ for $S_3$ and $3, 4$ for $S_4$, each $\equiv 1 \pmod p$, as the Third Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}) predicts.
\srcline{lecture 10/12; the counts are not from class}
\end{exbox}

\textit{(PS 6.4.)} The order of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ is found by counting bases of $\mathbb{F}_p^n$: an invertible matrix is the same as an ordered list of $n$ linearly independent columns.

\begin{lembox}[{Invertible Matrices Have Independent Columns}]\label{lem:invertible-iff-columns-independent}
Let $k$ be a field and $M \in \operatorname{Mat}_n(k)$ with columns $\vec m_1, \ldots, \vec m_n$. Then $M \in GL_n(k)$ if and only if $(\vec m_1, \ldots, \vec m_n)$ is linearly independent.
\srcline{not from class}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
For $\vec c = (c_1, \ldots, c_n) \in k^n$, $M\vec c = \sum_{i=1}^n c_i \vec m_i$. So the columns are linearly independent if and only if $M\vec c = \vec 0$ forces $\vec c = \vec 0$, that is, if and only if the linear map $\vec c \mapsto M\vec c$ of $k^n$ is injective. If $M$ is invertible, this map has the inverse $\vec c \mapsto M^{-1}\vec c$, so it is injective. Conversely, if it is injective, its kernel is $0$, so by rank--nullity its image has dimension $n$ and is all of $k^n$; the map is then a bijective linear map, its inverse is linear, and the matrix $N$ of the inverse satisfies $MN = NM = I_n$, so $M \in GL_n(k)$ (Definition~\ref{def:general-linear-groups-gln-k}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lembox}[{Counting Linearly Independent Tuples}]\label{lem:count-independent-tuples}
For $1 \leq k$, the number of linearly independent $k$-tuples $(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k)$ of vectors in $\mathbb{F}_p^n$ is
\[ (p^n - 1)(p^n - p) \cdots (p^n - p^{k-1}) . \]
\srcline{PS 6.4(1)}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $\vec v_{k+1}$ must avoid the span of $\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k$, which has $p^k$ elements.\par
By induction on $k$. Throughout, $|\mathbb{F}_p^n| = p^n$: each of the $n$ entries is one of the $p$ elements of $\mathbb{F}_p$.

\emph{Base case $k = 1$.} $(\vec v_1)$ is linearly independent if and only if $\vec v_1 \neq \vec 0$: if $\vec v_1 = \vec 0$, then $1 \cdot \vec v_1 = \vec 0$ is a nontrivial relation; if $\vec v_1 \neq \vec 0$ and $c\vec v_1 = \vec 0$ with $c \neq 0$, then $\vec v_1 = c^{-1}(c\vec v_1) = \vec 0$, a contradiction. So there are $p^n - 1$ such $1$-tuples.

\emph{Inductive step.} Assume the count for $k$. If $(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_{k+1})$ is linearly independent, so is $(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k)$ (a relation among the first $k$ is one among all $k+1$ with coefficient $0$ on $\vec v_{k+1}$). So it suffices to show: \emph{for each linearly independent $(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k)$ there are exactly $p^n - p^k$ vectors $\vec v_{k+1}$ with $(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_{k+1})$ linearly independent}; then the count for $k+1$ is the count for $k$ times $p^n - p^k$. Let $S = \operatorname{span}(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k)$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{$(\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_{k+1})$ is dependent if and only if $\vec v_{k+1} \in S$.} If $\vec v_{k+1} = \sum_i c_i\vec v_i$, then $\sum_i c_i \vec v_i - 1\cdot\vec v_{k+1} = \vec 0$ is a nontrivial relation. Conversely, let $\sum_i c_i\vec v_i + c\,\vec v_{k+1} = \vec 0$ be a nontrivial relation. If $c = 0$ it is a nontrivial relation among $\vec v_1, \ldots, \vec v_k$, which is impossible; so $c \neq 0$ and $\vec v_{k+1} = -\sum_i c^{-1}c_i\,\vec v_i \in S$.
    \item \emph{$|S| = p^k$.} The map $\mathbb{F}_p^k \to S$, $(c_1, \ldots, c_k) \mapsto \sum_i c_i\vec v_i$, is onto by the definition of the span, and one-to-one: $\sum_i c_i\vec v_i = \sum_i d_i\vec v_i$ gives $\sum_i (c_i - d_i)\vec v_i = \vec 0$, so $c_i = d_i$ by independence. Hence $|S| = |\mathbb{F}_p^k| = p^k$.
\end{enumerate}
So exactly $p^k$ of the $p^n$ vectors $\vec v_{k+1}$ give a dependent tuple, and $p^n - p^k$ give an independent one.
\end{proof}

For $k > n$ the product contains the factor $p^n - p^n = 0$, in agreement with there being no $n+1$ linearly independent vectors in $\mathbb{F}_p^n$.

\begin{propbox}[{The Order of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$}]\label{prop:order-gln-fp}
\[ |GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)| = p^{\binom{n}{2}} \prod_{k=1}^{n} (p^k - 1). \]
In particular a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ has order $p^{\binom{n}{2}}$, since $p \nmid p^k - 1$ for $k \geq 1$.
\srcline{stated on WS 10; PS 6}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Count ordered bases of $\mathbb{F}_p^n$, then factor $p^i$ out of each $p^n - p^i$.\par
\textit{(PS 6.4(2); the last step, rewriting the product, is not from the problem set.)} A matrix $M \in \operatorname{Mat}_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ is the same as the ordered list $(\vec m_1, \ldots, \vec m_n)$ of its columns, and $M \in GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ exactly when that list is linearly independent (Lemma~\ref{lem:invertible-iff-columns-independent}). So by Lemma~\ref{lem:count-independent-tuples} with $k = n$,
\[ |GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)| = (p^n - 1)(p^n - p) \cdots (p^n - p^{n-1}) = \prod_{i=0}^{n-1} (p^n - p^i) . \]
Each factor is $p^n - p^i = p^i(p^{n-i} - 1)$, so
\[ |GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)| = p^{0 + 1 + \cdots + (n-1)} \prod_{i=0}^{n-1} (p^{n-i} - 1) = p^{\binom{n}{2}} \prod_{k=1}^{n} (p^k - 1), \]
with $0 + 1 + \cdots + (n-1) = \binom{n}{2}$ and $k = n - i$. Finally $p \nmid p^k - 1$ for $k \geq 1$ (as $p \mid p^k$, it would divide $1$), so by unique factorization the largest power of $p$ dividing $|GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)|$ is $p^{\binom{n}{2}}$, which is the order of a Sylow $p$-subgroup (Definition~\ref{def:sylow-subgroup}).
\end{proof}

Worksheet 10 quotes this order from Problem Set 6; the proof above is the submitted solution of Problem 6.4.

\begin{propbox}[{The Unitriangular Matrices Are a Sylow $p$-Subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$}]\label{prop:unitriangular-sylow}
Let $P \subseteq GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ be the group of matrices of the form
\[ \begin{bmatrix} 1 & b_{12} & b_{13} & \cdots & b_{1(n-1)} & b_{1n} \\ 0 & 1 & b_{23} & \cdots & b_{2(n-1)} & b_{2n} \\ 0 & 0 & 1 & \cdots & b_{3(n-1)} & b_{3n} \\ & & & \ddots & & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 & b_{(n-1)n} \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}. \]
Then $P$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$.
\srcline{WS 10.2}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $P = I_n + \mathcal{N}$ with $\mathcal{N}$ nilpotent; count the $\binom{n}{2}$ free entries.\par
\textit{(Not from lecture; filled in.)} Write $\operatorname{Mat}_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ for the $n \times n$ matrices over $\mathbb{F}_p$, $I_n$ for the identity matrix, and
\[ \mathcal{N} = \{ N \in \operatorname{Mat}_n(\mathbb{F}_p) : N_{ij} = 0 \text{ whenever } i \geq j \} \]
for the strictly upper triangular matrices. A matrix has the displayed form exactly when it is $I_n + N$ with $N \in \mathcal{N}$ (the entries $b_{ij}$, $i < j$, are the entries of $N$ above the diagonal), so
\[ P = \{ I_n + N : N \in \mathcal{N} \} \subseteq \operatorname{Mat}_n(\mathbb{F}_p). \]
We show: \textbf{(1)} a product of $j$ matrices in $\mathcal{N}$ vanishes on and below the $(j-1)$-st diagonal above the main one; \textbf{(2)} $P$ contains $I_n$ and is closed under products; \textbf{(3)} every $A \in P$ is invertible with $A^{-1} \in P$, so $P$ is a subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$; \textbf{(4)} $|P| = p^{\binom{n}{2}}$; \textbf{(5)} $p^{\binom{n}{2}}$ is the order of a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$. Matrix products are computed by the entry formula $(AB)_{ab} = \sum_{r=1}^{n} A_{ar} B_{rb}$ (Definition~\ref{def:general-linear-groups-gln-k}).

\textbf{(1) Products of strictly upper triangular matrices.} \emph{Claim: if $j \geq 1$ and $N_1, \ldots, N_j \in \mathcal{N}$, then $(N_1 N_2 \cdots N_j)_{ab} = 0$ unless $b \geq a + j$.} By induction on $j$. For $j = 1$ this is the definition of $\mathcal{N}$: $(N_1)_{ab} = 0$ unless $b > a$, that is, unless $b \geq a + 1$. Assume the claim for $j$. Then
\[ (N_1 \cdots N_j N_{j+1})_{ab} = \sum_{r=1}^{n} (N_1 \cdots N_j)_{ar} \, (N_{j+1})_{rb}, \]
and the $r$-th term can be nonzero only if $r \geq a + j$ (induction hypothesis) and $b \geq r + 1$ (case $j = 1$), which together force $b \geq a + j + 1$. So the entry is $0$ unless $b \geq a + (j + 1)$, which proves the claim. Consequences:
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $N, N' \in \mathcal{N}$, then $NN' \in \mathcal{N}$, and $N^j \in \mathcal{N}$ for every $j \geq 1$: by the claim (with $j = 2$, resp.\ all $N_i = N$), a nonzero entry in position $(a, b)$ needs $b \geq a + 2 > a$, resp.\ $b \geq a + j > a$.
    \item If $N \in \mathcal{N}$, then $N^n = 0$: a nonzero entry in position $(a, b)$ would need $b \geq a + n \geq 1 + n$, impossible since $b \leq n$.
    \item $\mathcal{N}$ is closed under sums and scalar multiples, since these are computed entrywise and $0 + 0 = c \cdot 0 = 0$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{(2) Identity and closure.} $I_n = I_n + 0$ with $0 \in \mathcal{N}$ (all $b_{ij} = 0$), so $I_n \in P$. Let $A = I_n + N$ and $B = I_n + N'$ with $N, N' \in \mathcal{N}$. Expanding by distributivity of matrix multiplication,
\[ AB = (I_n + N)(I_n + N') = I_n + (N + N' + NN'), \]
and $N + N' + NN' \in \mathcal{N}$ by (1). So $AB \in P$.

\textbf{(3) Inverses.} Let $A = I_n + N \in P$ with $N \in \mathcal{N}$, and put ($N^0 = I_n$)
\[ B = I_n - N + N^2 - \cdots + (-1)^{n-1} N^{n-1} = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} (-1)^j N^j = I_n + \sum_{j=1}^{n-1} (-1)^j N^j. \]
The last sum lies in $\mathcal{N}$ by (1), so $B \in P$. The product telescopes, shifting the index $j + 1 \to j$ in the second sum and using $N^n = 0$ from (1):
\[ AB = B + NB = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} (-1)^j N^j + \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} (-1)^j N^{j+1} = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} (-1)^j N^j - \sum_{j=1}^{n} (-1)^j N^j = N^0 - (-1)^n N^n = I_n. \]
Since $N N^j = N^{j+1} = N^j N$, also $BN = NB$, so $BA = B + BN = B + NB = AB = I_n$. Hence $A$ is invertible with $A^{-1} = B \in P$; in particular $P \subseteq GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ (Definition~\ref{def:general-linear-groups-gln-k}). Together with (2), $P$ contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses, so $P$ is a subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ (Definition~\ref{def:subgroup}).

\textbf{(4) The order of $P$.} An element $I_n + N$ of $P$ is determined by the entries $b_{ij}$ of $N$ in the positions $(i, j)$ with $1 \leq i < j \leq n$; each of these may be any of the $p$ elements of $\mathbb{F}_p$, independently of the others, and different choices give different matrices. In row $i$ there are $n - i$ such positions ($j = i + 1, \ldots, n$), so the number of positions is
\[ \sum_{i=1}^{n} (n - i) = (n - 1) + (n - 2) + \cdots + 1 + 0 = \frac{n(n-1)}{2} = \binom{n}{2}, \]
and therefore $|P| = p^{\binom{n}{2}}$.

\textbf{(5) Comparison with $|GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)|$.} By The Order of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:order-gln-fp}),
\[ |GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)| = \prod_{i=0}^{n-1} (p^n - p^i) = p^{\binom{n}{2}}\, m, \qquad m = \prod_{k=1}^{n} (p^k - 1), \]
and $p \nmid m$: for each $k \geq 1$, $p \nmid p^k - 1$ (since $p \mid p^k$, it would divide $1$), so by Euclid's Lemma (Lemma~\ref{lem:euclids-lemma}), applied $n - 1$ times, $p$ does not divide the product $m$. So in the factorization $|GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)| = p^{\binom{n}{2}} m$ with $p \nmid m$, the exponent of $p$ is $\binom{n}{2}$, and a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ is a subgroup of order $p^{\binom{n}{2}}$ (Definition~\ref{def:sylow-subgroup}). By (3) and (4), $P$ is a subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ of order $p^{\binom{n}{2}}$, hence a Sylow $p$-subgroup.
\end{proof}

\begin{exbox}[{A Sylow $5$-Subgroup of $GL_2(\mathbb{F}_5)$}]\label{ex:sylow-gl2-f5}
$|GL_2(\mathbb{F}_5)| = (5^2 - 1)(5^2 - 5) = 24 \cdot 20 = 480 = 2^5 \cdot 3 \cdot 5$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:order-gln-fp}), so a Sylow $5$-subgroup has order $5$. The matrix $u = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ satisfies $u^j = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & j \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, so it has order $5$, and $\langle u \rangle$ is a Sylow $5$-subgroup. It is exactly the unitriangular group of WS 10.2 (Proposition~\ref{prop:unitriangular-sylow}) for $n = 2$, $p = 5$, which has $p^{\binom{2}{2}} = 5$ elements.
\srcline{lecture 10/14}
\end{exbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Groups of Order $p^2$}{Groups of Order p²}}\label{sub:order-p-squared}

\textit{(PS 6.3.)} A nontrivial central element, which every nontrivial $p$-group has, is enough to classify the groups of order $p^2$. The splitting step is a general criterion, isolated first.

\begin{lembox}[{Internal Direct Products (from PS 6.3(5))}]\label{lem:internal-direct-product}
Let $G$ be a finite group and $H, L \leq G$ subgroups with
\begin{enumerate}
    \item every element of $H$ commuting with every element of $L$,
    \item $H \cap L = \{e\}$, and
    \item $|H|\,|L| = |G|$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $\varphi: H \times L \to G$, $\varphi(h, l) = hl$, is an isomorphism. Condition (1) holds in particular if $H$ is central ($H \subseteq Z(G)$), or if $H$ and $L$ are both normal and (2) holds.
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $(h, l) \mapsto hl$ is an injective homomorphism between groups of equal size.\par
\emph{Homomorphism.} For $h, h' \in H$ and $l, l' \in L$, using $lh' = h'l$ from (1),
\[ \varphi\big((h, l)(h', l')\big) = \varphi(hh', ll') = hh'll' = hlh'l' = \varphi(h, l)\,\varphi(h', l') \]
(the product in $H \times L$ is componentwise, Definition~\ref{def:direct-products}). \emph{Injective.} If $\varphi(h, l) = hl = e$, then $h = l^{-1}$ lies in $H \cap L = \{e\}$, so $(h, l) = (e, e)$: the kernel is trivial. \emph{Bijective.} $|H \times L| = |H|\,|L| = |G|$, and an injective map between finite sets of the same size is a bijection. So $\varphi$ is an isomorphism.

\emph{The two sufficient conditions for (1).} If $H \subseteq Z(G)$ (Definition~\ref{def:center-z-g}), every $h \in H$ commutes with everything. If $H, L \trianglelefteq G$ and $H \cap L = \{e\}$, let $h \in H$, $l \in L$ and consider $hlh^{-1}l^{-1}$: grouped as $(hlh^{-1})\,l^{-1}$ it lies in $L$, since $hlh^{-1} \in L$ by normality of $L$; grouped as $h\,(lh^{-1}l^{-1})$ it lies in $H$, since $lh^{-1}l^{-1} \in H$ by normality of $H$. So $hlh^{-1}l^{-1} \in H \cap L = \{e\}$, i.e.\ $hl = lh$. \textit{(Not from the problem set: the problem used only the central case.)}
\end{proof}

\begin{thmbox}[{Groups of Order $p^2$}]\label{thm:groups-order-p-squared}
Let $p$ be prime and $|G| = p^2$. Then $G \cong \mathbb{Z}/p^2\mathbb{Z}$ or $G \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$.
\srcline{PS 6.3}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Take a central $z \neq e$; either $G$ is cyclic or $G \cong \langle z \rangle \times \langle g \rangle$.\par
Throughout: if $h \in G$ has finite order $c$, then $|\langle h \rangle| = c$ and $h^N = e$ iff $c \mid N$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:homomorphism-k-maps-gk-gen-g-iso-z-nz}); and \emph{every $h \neq e$ in $G$ has order $p$ or $p^2$}, since $|\langle h \rangle|$ divides $p^2$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}) and is not $1$.

By Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-power-order-have-nontrivial-center} (PS 3.5) there is $z \neq e$ with $zg = gz$ for all $g \in G$.

\emph{(1) If $z$ has order $p^2$.} Then $\langle z \rangle \subseteq G$ both have $p^2$ elements, so $G = \langle z \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}/p^2\mathbb{Z}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:homomorphism-k-maps-gk-gen-g-iso-z-nz}(3)). From now on $z$ has order $p$.

\emph{(2) $\langle z \rangle \trianglelefteq G$ and $G/\langle z \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$.} By induction on $k \geq 0$, $gz^k = z^kg$ for every $g \in G$: $gz^k = (gz^{k-1})z = z^{k-1}gz = z^{k-1}zg = z^kg$. So $g h g^{-1} = h$ for all $h \in \langle z \rangle = \{e, z, \ldots, z^{p-1}\}$, and $\langle z \rangle$ is normal (Definition~\ref{def:normal-subgroup}); the quotient $G/\langle z \rangle$ is a group (Definition~\ref{def:quotient-group}) of order $[G : \langle z \rangle] = p^2/p = p$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}), hence $\cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}, PS 2.5).

\emph{(3) A generator.} Let $\bar g$ generate $G/\langle z \rangle$ and let $g \in G$ with $g\langle z \rangle = \bar g$. As $\bar g$ generates a group of order $p$, it has order $p$, so $g^p\langle z \rangle = \bar g^{\,p} = \langle z \rangle$ and $g^p \in \langle z \rangle$: $g^p = z^m$, and writing $m = qp + a$ with $0 \leq a < p$, $g^p = (z^p)^q z^a = z^a$. Also $g \neq e$, since $\bar g \neq \langle z \rangle$ (the quotient has $p \geq 2$ elements).

\emph{(4) If $a \neq 0$.} Then $p \nmid a$, so $g^p = z^a \neq e$; $g$ has order $p$ or $p^2$, and not $p$, so order $p^2$, and $G = \langle g \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}/p^2\mathbb{Z}$ as in (1).

\emph{(5) If $a = 0$.} Then $g^p = e$, so $g$ has order $p$ and $\langle g \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} \cong \langle z \rangle$. The subgroup $\langle z \rangle \cap \langle g \rangle$ of $\langle g \rangle$ has order $1$ or $p$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}); order $p$ would give $\langle g \rangle \subseteq \langle z \rangle$, so $g \in \langle z \rangle$ and $\bar g = \langle z \rangle$, which is false. So $\langle z \rangle \cap \langle g \rangle = \{e\}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:internal-direct-product} with $H = \langle z \rangle$ (central) and $L = \langle g \rangle$, $|H|\,|L| = p \cdot p = |G|$, gives
\[ G \cong \langle z \rangle \times \langle g \rangle \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} . \qedhere \]
\end{proof}

\begin{corbox}[{Groups of Order $p^2$ Are Abelian}]\label{cor:order-p-squared-abelian}
Every group of order $p^2$, $p$ prime, is abelian.
\end{corbox}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-order-p-squared}, $G$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}/p^2\mathbb{Z}$ or to $\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$, both abelian (the product componentwise, Definition~\ref{def:direct-products}); and a group isomorphic to an abelian group is abelian, since $\varphi(ab) = \varphi(a)\varphi(b) = \varphi(b)\varphi(a) = \varphi(ba)$ and $\varphi$ is injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{Groups of Order $p^k$}]\label{rem:groups-order-pk}
Speyer, on Problem Set 6: one can continue classifying groups of order $p^k$ in this way for any particular $k$, although the number of cases grows rapidly; groups of order $p^3$ are ``tractable for an undergraduate''. The largest exponent fully classified is $k = 7$ (O'Brien and Vaughan-Lee, ``The groups with order $p^7$ for odd prime $p$'', \emph{Journal of Algebra}, 2005), with partial progress on $k = 8$ and $9$ published as recently as 2026.
\srcline{PS 6, stated, not proved}
\end{rembox}

\textit{$GL_n$ elsewhere: previous, the recap earlier in this chapter (\ref{sec:rev-ch9-gl}); no later appearance yet.}

\section{The Sylow Theorems}\label{sec:sylow-theorems}

\textit{Reference: Worksheet 10. Throughout, $p$ is a prime and the groups are finite.}

\begin{rembox}[{The Strategy}]\label{rem:sylow-strategy}
Every finite group $G$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of some $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ (Every Finite Group Is a Matrix Group, Corollary~\ref{cor:every-finite-group-matrix-group}), and $\Gamma = GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ has an explicit Sylow $p$-subgroup $\Pi$, the unitriangular matrices (WS 10.2, Proposition~\ref{prop:unitriangular-sylow}). The plan is to transfer Sylow subgroups from the large group $\Gamma$ down to its subgroup $G$, by letting $G$ act on $\Gamma/\Pi$ (WS 10.3, Proposition~\ref{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}). The lecture sketched the key step in the case where $G$ is itself a $p$-group: the next lemma.
\srcline{lecture 10/12}
\end{rembox}

\begin{lembox}[{$p$-Subgroups Lie in a Conjugate of a Sylow Subgroup}]\label{lem:p-subgroup-in-conjugate-sylow}
Let $\Gamma$ be a finite group with a Sylow $p$-subgroup $\Pi$, and let $P \leq \Gamma$ be a $p$-subgroup. Then there is $\gamma \in \Gamma$ with $P \subseteq \gamma \Pi \gamma^{-1}$.
\srcline{lecture 10/12}
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} $P$ acts on $\Gamma/\Pi$, of size prime to $p$, so it fixes a coset $\gamma\Pi$.\par
Write $|\Gamma| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$, so $|\Pi| = p^k$. Let $X = \Gamma/\Pi$; by Lagrange (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}), $|X| = [\Gamma : \Pi] = m \not\equiv 0 \pmod p$. Let $P$ act on $X$ by left multiplication, the restriction to $P$ of the action of $\Gamma$ on $\Gamma/\Pi$ (The Action of $G$ on $G/H$, Proposition~\ref{prop:action-g-g-h}). By WS 10.1 (Proposition~\ref{prop:p-group-fixed-point}), $P$ fixes some coset $\gamma\Pi \in X$, with $\gamma \in \Gamma$, i.e.\ $P \subseteq \operatorname{Stab}(\gamma\Pi)$. By part (3) of Proposition~\ref{prop:action-g-g-h}, $\operatorname{Stab}(\gamma\Pi) = \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$. Hence $P \subseteq \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corbox}[{$p$-Subgroups of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ Are Conjugate into Unitriangular Matrices}]\label{cor:p-subgroups-gln-unitriangular}
Let $Q$ be a $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ and $P$ the unitriangular group. Then $\gamma^{-1} Q \gamma \subseteq P$ for some $\gamma \in GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$.
\srcline{lecture 10/12}
\end{corbox}

\begin{proof}
By WS 10.2 (Proposition~\ref{prop:unitriangular-sylow}), $P$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:p-subgroup-in-conjugate-sylow} with $\Gamma = GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ and $\Pi = P$, $Q \subseteq \gamma P \gamma^{-1}$ for some $\gamma \in GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$; conjugating by $\gamma^{-1}$, $\gamma^{-1} Q \gamma \subseteq \gamma^{-1}(\gamma P \gamma^{-1})\gamma = P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{A Surprising Consequence}]\label{rem:sylow-surprising}
The corollary is not obvious from linear algebra. For instance, any subgroup with $16$ elements of $GL_{10^6}(\mathbb{F}_2)$, however its matrices look, becomes a group of upper triangular matrices after a single change of basis. The proof gives no formula for the change of basis; it comes from a counting argument.
\srcline{lecture 10/12}
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{Sylow Subgroups of a Subgroup}]\label{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}
Let $\Gamma$ be a finite group with a Sylow $p$-subgroup $\Pi$, and let $G$ be a subgroup of $\Gamma$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $G$ has a Sylow $p$-subgroup $P$.
    \item More specifically, there is some $\gamma \in \Gamma$ such that $P = G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{WS 10.3; class 10/14}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Some $G$-orbit in $\Gamma/\Pi$ has size prime to $p$; its stabilizer is Sylow in $G$.\par
\textit{(Class 10/14.)} Write $|\Gamma| = p^k m$ and $|G| = p^l n$ with $p \nmid m$ and $p \nmid n$, so $|\Pi| = p^k$; we must find a subgroup of $G$ of order $p^l$. Let $X = \Gamma/\Pi$, so $|X| = [\Gamma : \Pi] = m \not\equiv 0 \pmod p$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}). Let $G$ act on $X$ by left multiplication, the restriction to $G$ of the action of $\Gamma$ on its cosets.

\emph{An orbit of size prime to $p$.} $X$ breaks up into $G$-orbits (Proposition~\ref{prop:orbits-partition-x}), and $|X|$ is the sum of their sizes. Since $p \nmid |X|$, at least one orbit $G \cdot (\gamma\Pi)$, with $\gamma \in \Gamma$, has size $\not\equiv 0 \pmod p$.

\emph{Its stabilizer.} For $h \in G$, $h\gamma\Pi = \gamma\Pi$ iff $\gamma^{-1}h\gamma \in \Pi$ iff $h \in \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$. So
\[ \operatorname{Stab}_G(\gamma\Pi) = G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}. \]

\emph{Counting.} By orbit--stabilizer (Theorem~\ref{thm:orbit-stabilizer}),
\[ p^l n = |G| = |G \cdot (\gamma\Pi)| \cdot |G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}|. \]
The first factor is not divisible by $p$, so all $l$ factors of $p$ lie in the second: $p^l$ divides $|G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}|$. On the other hand, $G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$ is a subgroup of $\gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}$, which has order $|\Pi| = p^k$ (conjugation is an isomorphism, Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}), so by Lagrange $|G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1}| = p^j$ for some $j$ with $l \leq j \leq k$. It is also a subgroup of $G$, so $p^j$ divides $p^l n$, giving $j \leq l$. Hence $j = l$, and
\[ P := G \cap \gamma\Pi\gamma^{-1} \]
is a subgroup of $G$ of order $p^l$: a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$, of the form required in (2).
\end{proof}

\textit{Worksheet hint for (1): consider $G$ acting on $\Gamma/\Pi$. Worksheet hint for the next three results: use Proposition~\ref{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}.}

\begin{thmbox}[{The First Sylow Theorem}]\label{thm:sylow-first}
Every finite group $G$ has a Sylow $p$-subgroup.
\srcline{WS 10.4; class 10/14}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Embed $G$ in $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$, which has a Sylow $p$-subgroup, and apply WS 10.3.\par
\textit{(Class 10/14.)} Let $G$ be finite with $|G| = n$. By Every Finite Group Is a Matrix Group (Corollary~\ref{cor:every-finite-group-matrix-group}), $G$ is isomorphic to a subgroup $G'$ of $\Gamma = GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$, and $\Gamma$ has a Sylow $p$-subgroup $\Pi$, the unitriangular matrices (WS 10.2, Proposition~\ref{prop:unitriangular-sylow}). By WS 10.3(1) (Proposition~\ref{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}), $G'$ has a Sylow $p$-subgroup; its preimage under the isomorphism $G \to G'$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$. (The order of $GL_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$ that WS 10.2 uses is Proposition~\ref{prop:order-gln-fp}, PS 6.4.)
\end{proof}

\begin{propbox}[{$p$-Subgroups and Conjugates of a Sylow Subgroup}]\label{prop:p-subgroups-conjugates-sylow}
Let $G$ be a finite group and let $P$ be a Sylow $p$-subgroup with $|P| = p^k$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item If $Q$ is a $p$-subgroup of $G$, there is some $g \in G$ such that $Q \subseteq gPg^{-1}$.
    \item If $H$ is a subgroup of $G$ whose order is divisible by $p^k$, there is some $g \in G$ such that $H \supseteq gPg^{-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{WS 10.5; board 10/14}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} (1) is Lemma~\ref{lem:p-subgroup-in-conjugate-sylow} with $\Gamma = G$; (2) a Sylow subgroup of $H$ is a conjugate of $P$.\par
\textbf{(1)} This is Lemma~\ref{lem:p-subgroup-in-conjugate-sylow} with $\Gamma = G$, $\Pi = P$, and $Q$ in the role of its $P$; the conjugating element lies in $\Gamma = G$.

\textbf{(2) (board 10/14).} Since $H \leq G$, $|H|$ divides $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}); as $p^k$ divides $|H|$, the largest power of $p$ dividing $|H|$ is exactly $p^k$. By the First Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-first}), $H$ has a Sylow $p$-subgroup $Q$, of order $p^k$. Then $Q$ is a $p$-subgroup of $G$, so by (1) $Q \subseteq gPg^{-1}$ for some $g \in G$; both have $p^k$ elements, so $Q = gPg^{-1}$. Hence $H \supseteq Q = gPg^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof (directly from WS 10.3)]
\textit{Idea:} Apply WS 10.3(2) with $\Gamma = G$, $\Pi = P$ to the subgroups $Q$ and $H$.\par
\textit{(Not from lecture; from the student's worksheet solutions. This is the route of the worksheet hint.)} Write $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$. Apply Sylow Subgroups of a Subgroup (WS 10.3(2), Proposition~\ref{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}) with $\Gamma = G$ and $\Pi = P$: for every subgroup $L \leq G$ there is some $g \in G$ (an element of $\Gamma = G$) such that $L \cap gPg^{-1}$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $L$. Also $|gPg^{-1}| = |P| = p^k$, since conjugation by $g$ is a bijection (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}).

\textbf{(1)} Let $|Q| = p^j$. The largest power of $p$ dividing $|Q|$ is $p^j = |Q|$, so a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $Q$ is $Q$ itself. Taking $L = Q$ gives some $g \in G$ with $Q \cap gPg^{-1} = Q$, that is, $Q \subseteq gPg^{-1}$.

\textbf{(2)} By Lagrange (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}), $|H|$ divides $|G| = p^k m$, and $p \nmid m$, so $p^{k+1} \nmid |H|$; together with $p^k \mid |H|$, a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $H$ has order $p^k$. Taking $L = H$ gives some $g \in G$ with $|H \cap gPg^{-1}| = p^k = |gPg^{-1}|$. A subset of the finite set $gPg^{-1}$ with as many elements is all of it, so $H \cap gPg^{-1} = gPg^{-1}$, that is, $gPg^{-1} \subseteq H$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thmbox}[{The Second Sylow Theorem}]\label{thm:sylow-second}
Let $G$ be a finite group and let $P_1$ and $P_2$ be two Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$. Then there is some $g \in G$ such that $P_2 = gP_1g^{-1}$.
\srcline{WS 10.6; lecture 10/16}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} WS 10.3(2) with $\Gamma = G$, $\Pi = P_1$ puts $P_2$ inside some $gP_1g^{-1}$, $g \in G$.\par
\textit{(Lecture 10/16, ``by 10.3''.)} Let $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$, so $|P_1| = |P_2| = p^k$. Apply WS 10.3(2) (Proposition~\ref{prop:sylow-of-subgroup}) with $\Gamma = G$, $\Pi = P_1$, and the subgroup $P_2$ in the role of $G$: there is $g \in G$ such that $P_2 \cap gP_1g^{-1}$ is a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $P_2$. Since $|P_2| = p^k$, a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $P_2$ has $p^k$ elements, so it is $P_2$ itself:
\[ P_2 \cap gP_1g^{-1} = P_2, \qquad\text{that is,}\qquad P_2 \subseteq gP_1g^{-1}. \]
Conjugation by $g$ is an isomorphism (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}), so $|gP_1g^{-1}| = |P_1| = p^k = |P_2|$, and the inclusion is an equality: $P_2 = gP_1g^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corbox}[{A Normal Sylow Subgroup Is the Only One}]\label{cor:sylow-normal-unique}
Let $P$ be a Sylow $p$-subgroup of a finite group $G$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item If $P \trianglelefteq G$, then $P$ is the only Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$.
    \item Conversely, if $P$ is the only Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$, then $P \trianglelefteq G$.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{lecture 10/16}
\end{corbox}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{(1) (lecture).} Let $Q$ be any Sylow $p$-subgroup. By the Second Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-second}), $Q = gPg^{-1}$ for some $g \in G$, and $gPg^{-1} = P$ because $P$ is normal (Definition~\ref{def:normal-subgroup}). So $Q = P$.

\textbf{(2)} \textit{(Not stated in lecture; used in the example of order $15$ below.)} For $g \in G$, $gPg^{-1}$ is a subgroup with $|gPg^{-1}| = |P|$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}), so it is a Sylow $p$-subgroup, hence equal to $P$. So $gPg^{-1} = P$ for every $g \in G$: $P$ is normal.
\end{proof}

\begin{lembox}[{The Order of a Product of Subgroups}]\label{lem:order-product-subgroups}
Let $H$ and $L$ be finite subgroups of a group $G$, and $HL = \{hl : h \in H,\ l \in L\}$ (Definition~\ref{def:product-set-hn}). Then
\[ |HL| = \frac{|H|\,|L|}{|H \cap L|}. \]
\end{lembox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{(Not from lecture; filled in. The lecture uses the formula in the proof of the Third Sylow Theorem.)} Consider the surjective map $\mu : H \times L \to HL$, $\mu(h, l) = hl$. We show that every element of $HL$ has exactly $|H \cap L|$ preimages; then $|H \times L| = |HL| \cdot |H \cap L|$, which is the formula.

Fix $hl \in HL$. For $d \in H \cap L$, $(hd, d^{-1}l) \in H \times L$ and $\mu(hd, d^{-1}l) = hdd^{-1}l = hl$; distinct $d$ give distinct pairs. Conversely, if $\mu(h', l') = hl$, put $d = h^{-1}h'$. Then $d \in H$, and $d = h^{-1}h' = l\,l'^{-1}$ (from $h'l' = hl$), so $d \in L$; and $h' = hd$, $l' = d^{-1}l$. So the preimages of $hl$ are exactly the $|H \cap L|$ pairs $(hd, d^{-1}l)$, $d \in H \cap L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{thmbox}[{The Third Sylow Theorem}]\label{thm:sylow-third}
For any finite group $G$, the number of Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$ is congruent to $1 \bmod p$ (Definition~\ref{def:congruence-modulo-n}): it equals $1 + kp$ for some integer $k \ge 0$, i.e.\ it leaves remainder $1$ on division by $p$.
\srcline{stated on WS 10; lecture 10/16}
\end{thmbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Let $P$ act on the Sylow $p$-subgroups by conjugation; $P$ is its only fixed point.\par
\textit{(Lecture 10/16.)} Let $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$, and let $X$ be the set of Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$. $X$ is nonempty by the First Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-first}); fix $P \in X$.

\emph{The action.} $G$ acts on $X$ by conjugation, $g \star Q = gQg^{-1}$: $gQg^{-1}$ is a subgroup with $|gQg^{-1}| = |Q|$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}), so it lies in $X$, and the action axioms are checked as for the conjugation action on elements (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-action}). By the Second Sylow Theorem there is only one orbit. Restrict the action to $P$.

\emph{Counting modulo $p$.} $X$ breaks up into $P$-orbits (Proposition~\ref{prop:orbits-partition-x}). Each $P$-orbit has size dividing $|P| = p^k$ (Orbit--Stabilizer, Theorem~\ref{thm:orbit-stabilizer}), so size $p^\ell$ for some $\ell \geq 0$; it is $\equiv 0 \pmod p$ unless $\ell = 0$, that is, unless the orbit is a single fixed point. Writing $X^P = \{Q \in X : xQx^{-1} = Q \text{ for all } x \in P\}$ for the set of points fixed by $P$,
\[ |X| \equiv |X^P| \pmod p. \]
$P$ itself is in $X^P$: for $x \in P$, $xPx^{-1} = P$.

\emph{No other fixed point.} We show that if $Q \in X$ and $Q \neq P$, then $P$ does not fix $Q$. Assume for contradiction that $xQx^{-1} = Q$ for all $x \in P$.

\emph{Claim: $PQ$ is a subgroup of $G$.} For $x_1, x_2 \in P$ and $q_1, q_2 \in Q$,
\[ (x_1q_1)(x_2q_2) = (x_1x_2)\,(x_2^{-1}q_1x_2)\,q_2 , \]
with $x_1x_2 \in P$ and $x_2^{-1}q_1x_2 \in Q$ by the assumption (applied to $x_2^{-1} \in P$); so $PQ$ is closed under products. \textit{(Filled in:)} $e = ee \in PQ$, and for $x \in P$, $q \in Q$, $(xq)^{-1} = q^{-1}x^{-1} = x^{-1}\,(xq^{-1}x^{-1}) \in PQ$, again by the assumption. This proves the claim.

\emph{Its order.} $P \cap Q$ is a subgroup of $P$, so $|P \cap Q| = p^j$ with $j \leq k$ (Lagrange, Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}); and $j < k$, since $j = k$ would give $P = P \cap Q \subseteq Q$, hence $P = Q$ (both have $p^k$ elements). By the Order of a Product of Subgroups (Lemma~\ref{lem:order-product-subgroups}),
\[ |PQ| = \frac{|P|\,|Q|}{|P \cap Q|} = \frac{p^k \cdot p^k}{p^j} = p^{2k - j}, \qquad 2k - j > k. \]
But $p^k$ is the largest power of $p$ dividing $|G|$, so $|PQ| \nmid |G|$, contradicting Lagrange. So $P$ fixes no $Q \neq P$.

\emph{Conclusion.} $X^P = \{P\}$, so $|X| = 1 + (\text{sizes } p^\ell,\ \ell \geq 1, \text{ of the other orbits}) \equiv 1 \pmod p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corbox}[{The Number of Sylow Subgroups Divides $|G|$}]\label{cor:sylow-number-divides}
Let $G$ be finite, $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$, and let $n_p$ be the number of Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$. Then $n_p$ divides $|G|$; in fact $n_p$ divides $m$. Together with the Third Sylow Theorem: $n_p \mid m$ and $n_p \equiv 1 \pmod p$.
\srcline{lecture 10/16}
\end{corbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Orbit--stabilizer for the single conjugation orbit; the stabilizer of $P$ contains $P$.\par
By the Second Sylow Theorem, the conjugation action of $G$ on the set $X$ of Sylow $p$-subgroups (proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}) has one orbit, $X = G \star P$ for any $P \in X$. By Orbit--Stabilizer (Theorem~\ref{thm:orbit-stabilizer}), $|G| = n_p \cdot |\operatorname{Stab}_G(P)|$, so $n_p$ divides $|G|$ (lecture). \textit{(Not from lecture:)} $P \subseteq \operatorname{Stab}_G(P)$, since $xPx^{-1} = P$ for $x \in P$; so $p^k$ divides $|\operatorname{Stab}_G(P)|$ (Lagrange), and $n_p = p^k m / |\operatorname{Stab}_G(P)|$ divides $m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{exbox}[{The Sylow $5$-Subgroups of $S_5$}]\label{ex:sylow-5-s5}
$|S_5| = 120 = 2^3 \cdot 3 \cdot 5$, so a Sylow $5$-subgroup has order $5$. It is cyclic, generated by each of its $4$ non-identity elements (Groups of Prime Order, Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}), and an element of order $5$ in $S_5$ is a $5$-cycle. There are $4! = 24$ five-cycles: writing a $5$-cycle as $(1\,a\,b\,c\,d)$, it is determined by the order of $a, b, c, d$. Two distinct subgroups of order $5$ meet in a subgroup whose order divides $5$ and is not $5$, so only in $e$. Hence the $24$ five-cycles fall into disjoint groups of $4$, one for each Sylow $5$-subgroup:
\[ n_5 = \frac{24}{4} = 6 . \]
As the Sylow theorems require, $6 \equiv 1 \pmod 5$ and $6$ divides $120 / 5 = 24$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-number-divides}).
\srcline{lecture 10/16}
\end{exbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Groups of Order $pq$}{Groups of Order pq}}\label{sub:order-pq}

\textit{Lecture 10/16.} The first classification by the Sylow theorems: counting Sylow subgroups forces them to be normal, and two normal subgroups of coprime orders split the group.

\begin{exbox}[{Groups of Order $15$}]\label{ex:groups-order-15}
Let $|G| = 15 = 3 \cdot 5$. Then $G \cong \mathbb{Z}/15\mathbb{Z}$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{One Sylow $3$-subgroup.} $n_3$ divides $15$, so $n_3 \in \{1, 3, 5, 15\}$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-number-divides}), and $n_3 \equiv 1 \pmod 3$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}); only $1$ qualifies. So there is one Sylow $3$-subgroup $P_3$, and it is normal (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-normal-unique}(2)).
    \item \emph{One Sylow $5$-subgroup.} The same for $5$: of $1, 3, 5, 15$ only $1$ is $\equiv 1 \pmod 5$. So $P_5$ is unique and normal.
    \item \emph{Trivial intersection.} $|P_3 \cap P_5|$ divides $3$ and $5$ (Lagrange), so $P_3 \cap P_5 = \{e\}$.
    \item \emph{Splitting.} $P_3$ and $P_5$ are normal with trivial intersection and $|P_3|\,|P_5| = 15 = |G|$, so by Internal Direct Products (Lemma~\ref{lem:internal-direct-product}) $G = P_3P_5 \cong P_3 \times P_5$. Each $P_i$ has prime order, hence is cyclic (Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}):
    \[ G \cong \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/5\mathbb{Z} \cong \mathbb{Z}/15\mathbb{Z} \]
    by the Chinese Remainder Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:chinese-remainder-theorem}).
\end{enumerate}
So every group with $15$ elements is cyclic.
\srcline{lecture 10/16}
\end{exbox}

\begin{propbox}[{Groups of Order $pq$}]\label{prop:groups-order-pq}
Let $p < q$ be primes with $q \not\equiv 1 \pmod p$, and let $|G| = pq$. Then $G \cong \mathbb{Z}/pq\mathbb{Z}$.
\srcline{lecture 10/16: ``same for $|G| = pq$''}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} Sylow counting forces $n_p = n_q = 1$; the two normal cyclic Sylow subgroups split $G$.\par
\textit{(Filled in, following the case $|G| = 15$.)} The divisors of $pq$ are $1, p, q, pq$.

\emph{$n_q = 1$.} $n_q$ divides $pq$ and $n_q \equiv 1 \pmod q$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-number-divides}, Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}). Modulo $q$: $q \equiv pq \equiv 0$, and $p \not\equiv 1$ since $1 < p < q$. So $n_q = 1$.

\emph{$n_p = 1$.} $n_p$ divides $pq$ and $n_p \equiv 1 \pmod p$. Modulo $p$: $p \equiv pq \equiv 0$, and $q \not\equiv 1$ by hypothesis. So $n_p = 1$.

\emph{Conclusion.} The unique Sylow subgroups $P_p$ and $P_q$ are normal (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-normal-unique}(2)), of prime orders $p$ and $q$, so cyclic (Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}); $P_p \cap P_q = \{e\}$ because its order divides $p$ and $q$ (Lagrange); and $|P_p|\,|P_q| = pq = |G|$. By Internal Direct Products (Lemma~\ref{lem:internal-direct-product}) and the Chinese Remainder Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:chinese-remainder-theorem}, $\gcd(p, q) = 1$),
\[ G \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z} \cong \mathbb{Z}/pq\mathbb{Z}. \]
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{When $q \equiv 1 \pmod p$}]\label{rem:order-pq-hypothesis}
\textit{(Not from lecture.)} The hypothesis cannot be dropped: $|S_3| = 6 = 2 \cdot 3$ with $3 \equiv 1 \pmod 2$, and $S_3$ is not cyclic. There $n_3 = 1$ but $n_2 = 3$ (Example~\ref{ex:sylow-s3-s4}), so the Sylow $2$-subgroups are not normal and the splitting argument stops at the second step. What remains is a semidirect product: $S_3 \cong \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z} \rtimes \mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$ (Example~\ref{ex:s3-external-semidirect}).
\end{rembox}

Worksheet 10: ``Realistically, we'll wind up covering this in lecture.''

% SECTION: SIMPLE GROUPS OF SMALL ORDER (Fri Oct 23)
% ============================================================================
\section{Simple Groups of Small Order}\label{sec:small-simple-groups}

\textit{Source: Lecture and slides (Fri Oct 23). Reference: not covered in the Pinter chapters used so far.}

\begin{rembox}[{The Game}]\label{rem:simplicity-game}
Which numbers $n \leq 100$ are the order of a simple group? The slides list $n = 2, \ldots, 100$ and mark each $n$ with the test that rules it out: \emph{prime}; $\clubsuit$, a prime power (Proposition~\ref{prop:primes-prime-powers-simple}); $\diamondsuit p$, a prime $p$ for which $n_p = 1$ is forced (Corollary~\ref{cor:unique-sylow-not-simple}); $\blacksquare p$, a prime $p$ for which $|G|$ does not divide $n_p!$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-hom-sylow}). After these tests only $30$, $56$, $60$ and $90$ survive. The orders $30$, $56$ and $90$ are ruled out on the homework, and $60$ is the order of $A_5$.
\srcline{lecture 10/23}
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{Primes and Prime Powers}]\label{prop:primes-prime-powers-simple}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The trivial group is not simple.
    \item If $|G| = p$ is prime, then $G \cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ is simple.
    \item If $|G| = p^k$ with $p$ prime and $k \geq 2$, then $G$ is not simple.
\end{enumerate}
\srcline{lecture 10/23}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} for (3), the center is a nontrivial normal subgroup; it is proper unless $G$ is abelian, and abelian simple groups have prime order.\par
\textbf{(1)} By Definition~\ref{def:simple-group}: the trivial group has only one normal subgroup.

\textbf{(2)} A group of prime order is cyclic, hence $\cong \mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-order}), and it is simple by Theorem~\ref{thm:abelian-simple-groups}.

\textbf{(3)} The center $Z(G) = \{z \in G : zg = gz \text{ for all } g \in G\}$ (Definition~\ref{def:center-z-g}) is nontrivial by Theorem~\ref{thm:groups-prime-power-order-have-nontrivial-center}, and it is normal in $G$: for $z \in Z(G)$ and $g \in G$, $gzg^{-1} = z$. If $Z(G) \neq G$, then $Z(G)$ is a proper nontrivial normal subgroup, and $G$ is not simple. If $Z(G) = G$, then $G$ is abelian, and an abelian simple group has prime order (Theorem~\ref{thm:abelian-simple-groups}); but $|G| = p^k$ with $k \geq 2$ is not prime. Either way $G$ is not simple.
\end{proof}

\begin{rembox}[{Notation for This Section}]\label{rem:small-simple-notation}
Throughout, $G$ is finite, $p$ is a prime dividing $|G|$, and $|G| = p^k m$ with $p \nmid m$. Write $n_p$ for the number of Sylow $p$-subgroups of $G$. The conjugate $gPg^{-1}$ of a Sylow $p$-subgroup $P$ is again a Sylow $p$-subgroup, since conjugation by $g$ is an isomorphism (Proposition~\ref{prop:conjugation-automorphism}); so $G$ acts on the set of Sylow $p$-subgroups by conjugation, $g \cdot P = gPg^{-1}$, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}.
\end{rembox}

\begin{defbox}[{Normalizer}]\label{def:normalizer}
Let $H \leq G$. The \textbf{normalizer} of $H$ in $G$ is
\[ N_G(H) = N(H) := \{ g \in G : gHg^{-1} = H \}, \]
the stabilizer of $H$ under the action of $G$ on its subgroups by conjugation.
\srcline{lecture 10/23}
\end{defbox}

\begin{rembox}[{Basic Properties of the Normalizer}]\label{rem:normalizer-properties}
$N(H)$ is a subgroup of $G$, since stabilizers are subgroups (Proposition~\ref{prop:stabilizer-subgroup}); it contains $H$, since $hHh^{-1} = H$ for $h \in H$; and $H \trianglelefteq N(H)$, directly from the definition. $H$ is normal in $G$ (Definition~\ref{def:normal-subgroup}) if and only if $N(H) = G$.
\end{rembox}

\begin{propbox}[{$n_p$ and the Normalizer}]\label{prop:np-normalizer-index}
Let $P$ be a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $G$. Then $n_p = [G : N(P)]$, and
\[ m = [N(P) : P] \cdot n_p. \]
In particular $n_p$ divides $m$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:sylow-number-divides}). Together with the Third Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}), $n_p \mid m$ and $n_p \equiv 1 \pmod p$.
\srcline{lecture 10/23}
\end{propbox}

\begin{proof}
\textit{Idea:} orbit--stabilizer for the single conjugation orbit, whose stabilizer is $N(P) \supseteq P$.\par
\textit{(Board 10/23.)} Let $G$ act on the set of Sylow $p$-subgroups by conjugation. By the Second Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-second}), any two Sylow $p$-subgroups are conjugate, so there is only one orbit, of size $n_p$. The stabilizer of $P$ is $\operatorname{Stab}(P) = \{g \in G : gPg^{-1} = P\} = N(P)$ (Definition~\ref{def:normalizer}). By Orbit--Stabilizer (Theorem~\ref{thm:orbit-stabilizer}), $n_p = [G : N(P)]$, and using Lagrange (Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}) for $P \leq N(P)$,
\[ p^k m = |G| = |N(P)| \cdot n_p = |P| \cdot [N(P) : P] \cdot n_p = p^k \, [N(P) : P] \cdot n_p. \]
Cancelling $p^k$ gives $m = [N(P) : P] \cdot n_p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Alternative proof that $n_p \mid m$ (number theory)]
\textit{(Board 10/23.)} From $n_p = [G : N(P)]$, $n_p$ divides $|G| = p^k m$. By the Third Sylow Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-third}) $n_p \equiv 1 \pmod p$, so $p \nmid n_p$; hence no factor of $p$ occurs in $n_p$, and $n_p$ divides $m$.
\end{proof}

Right: $G$ permutes the Sylow $p$-subgroups $P_1, \ldots, P_{n_p}$ transitively by conjugation (Theorem~\ref{thm:sylow-second}); the stabilizer of $P_1$ is $N(P_1)$, so the orbit has $[G : N(P_1)]$ points.
